Executive Summary: The key technology gap preventing a vertically stacked dual-bed
particle suspension reactor from achieving the DOE MYRD&D ultimate cost target for H2
production remains the lack of materials in particle form factor that exhibit ≥10% solar-to-
H2 energy conversion (STH) efficiency as a suspension. Therefore, our project goals
centered around strategies to increase the STH efficiency by enhancing photophysical
properties of perovskite oxide particles including increased visible-light absorption,
increased selectivity for electrocatalysis of the H2 evolution reaction (HER) and the O2
evolution reaction (OER) through development of ultrathin oxide coatings, correlating
composition and structure to function, and improving understanding of multiscale
transport and kinetic processes. Our research adds to the understanding of photocatalytic
reactors for solar water splitting through experiments on individual particles and
ensembles guided by insights from computational models and numerical simulations.
Reproducible synthesis and performance of efficient doped metal-oxide photocatalyst
particles (predominantly based on doped SrTiO3 and BiVO4) and ultrathin oxide coatings
for selective HER and OER redox (based on SiOx and TiOx) were demonstrated, and
quantitative results were used as inputs to numerical models of single particles and their
ensembles to predict pathways to ≥10% STH efficiency. Sets of data for composition–
structure–function were also obtained for composite photocatalyst materials, and a
comprehensive integrated numerical model was used to predict conditions required to
achieve large STH efficiencies. This coupled modeling–experiment workflow was also
used on developed reactor prototypes to identify conditions where temperature-gradient-
induced natural convection can achieve transport rates necessary to sustain a ≥10% STH
efficiency. Ultrathin oxide coatings deposited using atomic layer deposition on
photocatalyst particles were also shown to increase selectivity for the desired redox
reactions. Collectively, these results help identify technoeconomic constraints associated
with vertically stacked dual-bed particle suspension reactors that will enable optimization
of future designs. This work, and our results, also inform the public of the effectiveness
and promise of solar photocatalytic water splitting for green hydrogen production.

Background: According to technoeconomic analyses commissioned by the US DOE in
2009, particle suspension reactors are economically the most promising technology for
solar photoelectrochemical green H2 production.1 For H2 at the plant gate to be cost-
competitive with H2 produced from fossil fuels in the US, it was projected that particle
suspension reactors (Types 1 and 2) must exhibit ~10% STH efficiency, whose reactors
resemble algal bio-fermentation reactors except that algae is replaced by inexpensive
light-absorbing semiconductor metal-oxide nanoparticles. This STH efficiency
requirement is significantly smaller than that required for reactors based on light-
absorbing semiconductor wafers (Types 3 or 4) of ≥25% STH efficiency.1 By utilizing
specifically two compartments, the Type 2 (Z-Scheme) particle suspension reactor design
can separate locations of the light-driven HER and the light-driven OER. While intrinsically
safe with respect to H2 and O2 reactivity, this design necessitates inclusion of a soluble
redox mediator to shuttle electronic and ionic charge between the compartments via a
redox-active electrolyte.2 As part of our previous DOE–EERE-funded Incubator Project,
with the main aim to validate particle suspension reactor designs for solar water splitting
and identify suitable redox mediators, we investigated the advantages of a new Z-Scheme
design whereby reactor compartments were stacked vertically on top of each other3
instead of positioned side-by-side as modeled in the 2009 technoeconomic analyses.
Using technoeconomic analyses performed by our team with DOE H2A tools we projected
a 35% cost reduction of H2 at the plant gate for the stacked-bed design in comparison to
the side-by-side design. This projected decrease in the cost of H2 was predominantly due
to removal of PVC pipes, which had constituted the largest fraction of the projected capital
cost1 and were included for the side-by-side design to achieve mixing of redox-active
electrolyte via flow between the compartments over meters-long horizontal distances. We
determined that redox mediator transport over the centimeters-long vertical distances in
the stacked-bed design could be sustained by passive diffusion only, even for a device
operating at ≥10% STH efficiency.3 The projected cost of H2 at the plant gate was
determined to be $1.80/kg-H2, which was below the DOE MYRD&D ultimate cost target
at the time. Our analysis assumed baseline conditions supplied by DOE, plus (i) removal
of costs associated with forced convection of the redox-active electrolyte, (ii) reactor
compartments as large as modeled for the Type 1 reactor design, and (iii) a 10% STH
efficiency. Collectively, this design supports FOA requirements herein for particle
suspension reactors with a demonstrable pathway to achieving MYRD&D ultimate targets
at the time for efficiency (10% STH) and cost (<$2/kg-H2). While we are not aware of
technoeconomic analyses published by other groups on particle suspension reactors for
solar H2 production, system-level demonstrations as large as 100 m2 have been
reported.4 While this places the technology readiness level of the general particle
suspension reactor approach at ~6–7, this demonstration is of a Type 1 reactor design
that inherently produces a potentially explosive oxyhydrogen gas mixture due to light-
driven production of H2 and O2 and a single reactor compartment. By focusing exclusively
on Type 2 particle suspension reactor designs, our approach can mitigate formation of
such dangerous gas mixtures, while leveraging these synergistic efforts to accelerate the
timeline and improve the quality of our project.

                                       Page 4 of 127

Project Objectives: By progressing our understanding and development of designs for
green H2 based on a Type 2 particle suspension reactor that can be both economical and
intrinsically safe, our project is helping the U.S. to achieve national goals of clean energy
progression and economic benefit. Fundamental advances toward these impacts were
achieved via innovative approaches that led to specific reproducible demonstrations of a
≥0.1% quantum yield for H2 evolution and >90% stability over 1 day from suspensions of
newly discovered Ir-doped SrTiO3 photocatalyst particles under 750 – 850 nm near-
infrared-light illumination, and several ultrathin oxide coatings on thin-film electrodes that
each exhibited ≥95% selectivity for the desired HER and also the desired OER, over
undesired redox reactions with state-of-the-art redox mediators. These performance
metrics demonstrate progress along a pathway toward particle suspensions that absorb
a wide range of the solar spectrum and with high redox selectivity for water splitting
reactions that should enable a suspension reactor with a ≥3% STH efficiency. These
outcomes were achieved via a Statement of Project Objectives (SOPO) workplan that
consisted of four tasks, each with associated project-wide goals based on Milestones,
Go/No-Go Decision Points, and an End of Project Goal.
The major outcome for Task 1 (Photocatalyst Particles) was the demonstration of
quantum yields for the HER and the OER using visible-to-near-infrared light excitation of
ensembles of photocatalyst particles. An interim goal as the target for the Go/No-Go
Decision Point at the end of Year 1 – which was met – was to reproducibly (≥3 batches)
demonstrate a ≥0.1% quantum yield for H2 evolution and >90% stability over 1 day from
suspensions of newly discovered Ir-doped SrTiO3 photocatalyst particles (optionally
loaded with cocatalysts) in the presence of sacrificial electron donor species and under
750 – 850 nm near-infrared-light illumination. While we also fully met 15 other project-
wide Milestones along the way, we only completed 60% of the Milestone aimed at
synthesizing ≥5 types of mixed metal-oxide/nitride and/or metal-oxide/sulfide particles
that absorb light into the near-infrared spectral region at ≥800 nm. Notwithstanding,
important accomplishments that coincide with the photocatalyst particle goals and
objectives of the project – and which were all met – include that we demonstrated >1%
external quantum yield for O2 evolution from suspensions of BiVO4 photocatalyst particles
using visible-light illumination and >0.1% external quantum yield for H2 evolution – which
corresponds to a 1.4 – 2.1% internal quantum yield for H2 evolution – from suspensions
of Ir-doped SrTiO3 photocatalyst particles using near-infrared-light illumination. We also
worked with contractors and collaborators to obtain arrays of >1,000 doped SrTiO3
materials with various combinations of Rh, La, Ir, and Ba dopants, and strongly visible-
light-absorbing (CuGa)0.8Zn0.4S2 and CuGaS2 metal sulfide photocatalyst particles,
BaTaO2N mixed metal-oxide/nitride photocatalyst particles, and high-quality hybrid
organic–inorganic perovskite nanocrystals.
The major outcome for Task 2 (Selective Coatings) was the demonstration of several
ultrathin oxide coatings that resulted in high selectivity for the HER or the OER over redox
shuttle reactions, and their ability to be ported to photocatalyst particles. An interim goal
as the target for the Go/No-Go Decision Point at the end of Year 2 – which was met –
was to reproducibly (≥3 batches) demonstrate ≥1 selective ultrathin oxide coating on a
thin-film electrode that exhibits ≥95% selectivity for the desired HER and also the desired
OER, over undesired redox reactions with state-of-the-art redox mediators, which should
enable a ≥10 times larger quantum yield for the HER from coated doped-SrTiO3 materials

                                        Page 5 of 127

versus uncoated doped-SrTiO3 materials, consistent with a ≥3% STH efficiency. While
the ambitious End of Project Goal to reproducibly (≥3 batches) demonstrate ≥1 selective
ultrathin oxide coating on particles that results in a ≥10 times larger quantum yield for the
HER from coated doped-SrTiO3 particles versus uncoated doped-SrTiO3 particles,
consistent with a ≥3% STH efficiency was not met, other important accomplishments that
coincide with the selective coating goals and objectives of the project – and which were
all met – include that we deposited silicon oxide (SiOx) and titanium oxide (TiOx) coatings
by two methods and demonstrated their ability on thin-film electrodes consisting of Pt or
IrOx to exhibit >95% selectivity toward the HER over undesired Fe(III) of IO3– reduction
and the OER over undesired Fe(II) oxidation. We also performed scanning
electrochemical microscopy on isolated Pt disc electrodes and glassy carbon particles
each encapsulated by 5 – 10 nm thick SiOx overlayers and wetted by liquid electrolyte to
identify the influence of defects on redox selectivity. We also deposited ultrathin TiOx
coatings (2 nm, 3 nm, 10 nm) on Rh-doped SrTiO3 photocatalyst particles using an atomic
layer deposition method and we inferred a replicable greater than two-fold savings in
quantum yield for H2 evolution by the addition of ultrathin oxide coatings.
The major outcome for Task 3 (Experimental Techniques) was the development of
several correlative microscopy techniques to characterize single particles and high-
throughput experimentation workflows to measure arrays of 1,000 of materials samples.
Important accomplishments that coincide with the experimental techniques goals and
objectives of the project – and which were all met – include that we assessed >10 single-
particles of Rh-doped SrTiO3 for their electrical properties via current–voltage and
electron-beam-induced current measurements in the dry state and identified electron-
beam-induced currents that are consistent with those expected under solar illumination
conditions, where at least partial trap-state filling was observed to occurs and mobile
charge-carrier transport changes from a trap-mediated process to quasi-ohmic
conduction. We correlated these data with scanning transmission electron microscopy
images and associated spectroscopies, including beamline X-ray spectroscopy
measurements, as well as photoelectrochemical behavior when wetted by electrolyte. We
also electrically characterized single-crystal BiVO4 nanoparticles and found a difference
in Schottky barrier height between facets, as well as quantitatively analyzed the
correlation between photoluminescence and photocurrent for a single wetted BiVO4
particle. We also developed a high-throughput characterization workflow and used it to
scan an array of doped SrTiO3 materials with a throughput of 80 seconds per material,
with capabilities to screen the photocatalytic activity of the large array of >1000 materials
spots generated within one day. Results from fluorescence microscopy showed that
materials with larger Sr:Ti ratios added during synthesis and/or higher dopant densities
exhibited less photoluminescence at open circuit when wetted with a redox inactive
aqueous electrolyte. Additionally, photoelectrochemical measurements were performed
using mesoporous thin films of Rh-doped SrTiO3 particles, and a time-resolved
microwave conductivity spectroscopy system was developed to characterize mobile
charge-carrier mobility and lifetime in dry photocatalyst particles cast as thin films.
The major outcome for Task 4 (Numerical Models) were numerical models that spanned
single particles, to ensemble-level load line analyses, to bulk solution thermally-induced
convection. Important accomplishments that coincide with the numerical modeling goals
and objectives of the project – and which were all met – include that we developed and

                                        Page 6 of 127

validated a single-particle-scale model for individual photocatalyst particles and results
revealed that larger particles result in decreased predicted internal quantum yields. We
also modified the equivalent circuit modeling framework that is commonly used in the
photoelectrochemistry community to quantitatively account for the effects of two redox
reactions undergoing desired and undesired reactions with mobile electronic carriers in
the semiconductor, experimental ultrathin oxide coating properties, and the effects of
having an ensemble of independently operating photocatalyst particles, and used the
model to determine conditions that are predicted to result in 3%, 5% and 10% STH
efficiencies. We also designed and built a simplified reactor prototype to assess the extent
that solar-thermally-driven convection can provide an additional means of mass transfer
for redox shuttle species, where results indicate that the temperature gradient directly
correlates with light intensity and is significantly larger near the bottom surface of the
prototype than in the bulk solution. We also developed a reactor-scale modeling
framework with coupled flow, heat transfer, and mass transfer to assess the influence
that natural convection has on transport of redox mediator species within a single reaction
compartment and between two Z-Scheme reaction compartments in the presence of a
membrane separator. The model was used to determine the minimum concentrations of
redox shuttles that are needed to operate at specific STH efficiencies (i.e. 1%, 3%, 5%,
10%), where results point to clear benefits in terms of enhanced mass transfer in the
presence of natural convection.

Project Results and Discussion
Task 1. Photocatalyst Particles
Significant Accomplishments and Conclusions
• Using 3 different photocatalyst particle suspensions we reproducibly demonstrated >1%
  external quantum yield for O2 evolution from suspensions of BiVO4 photocatalyst particles in
  the presence of AgNO3 sacrificial electron acceptor species and under 405 nm visible-light
  illumination.
• Using 3 different photocatalyst particle suspensions we reproducibly demonstrated >0.1%
  external quantum yield for H2 evolution – which corresponds to a 1.4 – 2.1% internal quantum
  yield for H2 evolution – from suspensions of Ir-doped SrTiO3 photocatalyst particles containing
  Ir cocatalysts in the presence of CH3OH sacrificial electron donor species and under 785 nm
  near-infrared-light illumination.
• The Caltech contractor (Dr. John Gregoire) ink-jet deposited arrays of up to ~1,800 spots on
  quartz substrates, each consisting of doped SrTiO3 materials with various combinations of Rh,
  La, Ir, and Ba dopants. He also deposited arrays on crystalline silicon wafers with Ir or Rh
  dopants only, which are important because they are easier to dice into smaller test samples
  for rapid characterization using new high-throughput methods developed through this project.
  An initial screening of the array of materials was performed using electronic absorption
  spectroscopy and X-ray techniques.
• The Shinshu University contractor (Prof. Takashi Hisatomi) synthesized BaTaO2N mixed
  metal-oxide/nitride photocatalyst particles for this project, which have been reported to be
  efficient oxynitride photocatalysts for the HER and the OER upon absorption of <660 nm
  photons.
• The Tokyo University of Science contractor (Prof. Akihiko Kudo) synthesized (CuGa)0.8Zn0.4S2
  and CuGaS2 metal sulfide photocatalyst particles for this project, which have been reported
  to be efficient sulfide photocatalyst for the HER, albeit less stable than oxide-containing
  materials. This material, loaded with Ru cocatalysts, was designed to be coated in an ultrathin
  oxide coating by atomic layer deposition once we learned more about how to selectively
  control the spatial deposition of coatings, which could potentially result in a selective coating
  between Ru metal and sulfur/thiol surfaces. He also synthesized doped Ir-doped SrTiO3, Rh-
  doped SrTiO3, and BiVO4 photocatalyst particles for this project.
• An unfunded collaborator (Prof. Kazunari Domen at The University of Tokyo / Shinshu
  University) synthesized cocatalyst-modified Al-doped SrTiO3 photocatalyst particles for this
  project so that the single-particle electrical and electrochemical performance of these world-
  record, but only ultraviolet-light-absorbing, photocatalyst particles (with an external quantum
  yield (EQY) of approximately unity at ~360 nm) could be compared with those of BiVO4 and
  Rh-doped and Ir-doped SrTiO3.
• An unfunded collaborator (Dr. Joey Luther at NREL (which we identify as NLR herein due to
  the recent name change)) synthesized high-quality hybrid organic–inorganic perovskite
  nanocrystals for this project so that the role that ultrathin coatings can play in aqueous stability
  and redox selectivity could be determined.

Subtask 1.1. Assessment of Known Photocatalyst Particles. In accordance with
DOE’s Public Access Plan and NLR’s Data Catalog initiative, we compiled a database of
materials received from contractors and collaborators, and created an electronic log for
mass spectrometry data, as well as all project-wide data. Project members were also
trained on use of the HydroGEN data hub.

suspensions of Ir-doped SrTiO3 photocatalyst particles synthesized by a solid-state

and under near-infrared-light illumination (785 nm) from a high-power near-infrared laser
diode (Figure 4a), and for 3 separate suspensions. We also demonstrated >90% stability
over 1 day (>8 h) of continuous illumination, for 3 separate suspensions. We also
quantified the wavelength-dependent EQY, which is also known as the spectral response,
of the suspension using low-power LED illumination sources (Figure 4b). These data did
not show a measurable H2 signal above noise using near-infrared-light illumination
sources (i.e. 787 nm and 832 nm), which we suspect was because, in comparison to
other experiments, the incident light intensity was significantly lower, the concentration of
particles was about half as large, and the baseline pressure was about twice as large.

with our results of Ir-doped SrTiO3
being photoactive out to ~800 nm.
Rh-doped SrTiO3 exhibited the
second-most-intense absorption
that persisted out to >620 nm (<2
eV) and suggests that Rh dopants
are in the 4+ oxidation state, which
is known to result in a purple
coloration but no HER photoactivity.

flow of forming gas (5% v/v H2 in
N2), which we have observed
changes the coloration to yellow,
which is consistent with Rh being in

Further optimization enabled deposition of
six more high-quality arrays, this time on
crystalline silicon wafers, and consisting of
~200 combinations of SrTiO3 materials
doped with Ir or Rh only (Figure 10). The
change of substrate from quartz to silicon
aided sample preparation and produced
higher fidelity samples, because silicon has
better ink–substrate adhesion, and the
samples were easier to dice into smaller
pieces for rapid evaluation of possible
methods for high-throughput screening. Rh
and Ir dopants were chosen as benchmarks
for screening because of their large
photocatalytic activities for H2 evolution as Figure 10. Digital photograph of several high-

respectively. different conditions.
Synthesis of visible-to-near-infrared-light-absorbing mixed metal-oxide/nitride and/or
metal-oxide/sulfide particles. The Shinshu Team at Shinshu University synthesized
BaTaO2N photocatalyst particles, which have been reported previously to affect the HER
and the OER using >650 nm light excitation, albeit with a low apparent quantum yield.
Moreover, in addition to several batches of BiVO4, Ir-doped SrTiO3, and Rh-doped SrTiO3
particles, the Tokyo University of Science contractor synthesized metal–sulfide
photocatalyst particles, including (CuGa)0.8Zn0.4S2 and CuGaS2 materials, which have
also been shown to be active for the visible-light-driven HER but are inherently less stable
than their (mixed) metal-oxide counterparts. Additionally, as metal–sulfide photocatalyst
particles are generally p-type semiconductors, adding an n-type TiOx coating might create
a favorable pn-junction to facilitate charge separation, increase quantum yields for H2
evolution, and enhance stability. The aim would be to selectively deposit TiOx coatings
on sulfur/thiol surface groups instead of Ru metal. We also obtained a batch of hybrid
organic–inorganic perovskite nanocrystals from unfunded collaborators who are experts
in their synthesis and exhibit large quantum yields for photon emission and sufficient
visible-light absorption. We also received batches of cocatalyst-modified Al-doped SrTiO3
photocatalyst particles from unfunded collaborators. While these materials only absorb
ultraviolet light they exhibit near-unity EQY at ~360 nm and so the Talin SNL EMN Node
could compare their electrical properties to those of our visible-light-absorbing doped
SrTiO3 photocatalyst materials to help clarify the role of electrical properties in their light-
driven H2 evolution ability.

                                         Page 14 of 127

Task 2. Selective Coatings
Significant Accomplishments and Conclusions
• Greater than 3 times, we deposited silicon oxide (SiOx) and titanium oxide (TiOx) coatings by
  a wet chemical method or atomic layer deposition to a uniform thicknesses of 2 – 10 nm and
  sub-nanometer roughness onto thin-film electrodes consisting of Pt, a known catalyst for the
  HER and the OER.
• At moderate overpotentials, we demonstrated, at least 2 times, that in the presence of 25 mM
  of Fe(III) and Fe(II), ultrathin 5 – 10 nm thick SiOx or TiOx coatings on thin-film electrodes
  consisting of Pt exhibit >95% selectivity toward the HER over undesired Fe(III) reduction.
  Near-optimal coating thicknesses capable of achieving these selectivities for the HER exhibit
  a less than ~20% reduction in the partial current density for the HER relative to current
  densities measured for bare Pt electrodes, as supported molecular dynamics simulations.
• We performed scanning electrochemical microscopy on isolated Pt disc electrodes and glassy
  carbon particles each encapsulated by 5 – 10 nm thick SiOx overlayers and wetted by liquid
  electrolyte, specifically focusing on characterizing variations in reaction selectivity associated
  with single wetted particle defects that appear on these electrodes.
• At moderate overpotentials, we demonstrated, at least 2 times, that in the presence of 5 mM
  IO3– and I–, ultrathin 10 nm thick SiOx coatings on thin-film electrodes consisting of Pt exhibit
  >95% selectivity toward the HER over undesired IO3– reduction. Near-optimal coating
  thicknesses capable of achieving these selectivities for the HER exhibit a less than ~20%
  reduction in the partial current density for the HER relative to current densities measured for
  bare Pt electrodes.
• At moderate overpotentials, we demonstrated, at least 2 times, that in the presence of 3 – 25
  mM of Fe(III) and Fe(II), ultrathin 10 nm thick SiOx coatings on thin-film electrodes consisting
  of IrOx’ exhibit >95% selectivity toward the OER over undesired Fe(II) oxidation. Near-optimal
  coating thicknesses capable of achieving these selectivities for the OER exhibit a less than
  ~20% reduction in the partial current density for the OER relative to current densities
  measured for bare IrOx’ electrodes.
• Ultrathin TiOx coatings (2 nm, 3 nm, 10 nm) were deposited on Rh-doped SrTiO3 photocatalyst
  particles using an atomic layer deposition method, and these composite particles were
  assessed for photocatalytic HER activity in the presence of an aqueous methanol sacrificial
  donor and aqueous Fe(II) and/or Fe(III) redox shuttle species. In all cases, the external
  quantum yield (EQY) for H2 evolution was determined to be smaller than that for particles that
  did not have coatings. We also verified that the overlayers are permeable to methanol, as
  required to regenerate the photocatalyst by scavenging valence-band holes.
• By comparing photocatalytic rates and quantum yields for H2 evolution from aqueous
  suspensions of Rh-doped SrTiO3 nanoparticles in the presence of aqueous Fe(II) only versus
  with added Fe(III), with and without ultrathin TiOx coatings, for several solar-relevant incident
  light intensities and several batches of samples, we inferred a replicable greater than two-fold
  savings in quantum yield for H2 evolution by the addition of ultrathin oxide coatings, likely due
  to attenuation of undesired Fe(III) reduction by conduction-band electrons due to poorer
  permeation of Fe(III) in the coatings. We also performed additional mass spectrometric
  experiments that suggest that possible sample aging during typical experimental conditions
  (i.e. 24 hours of reaction time) is negligible, and the previous conclusion that ultrathin oxide
  coatings provide enhanced reaction selectivity for H2 evolution over Fe(III) reduction is likely
  not affected by possible sample aging issues.

                                          Page 15 of 127

Subtask 2.1. Coatings on Thin-Film Electrodes. This work focused on assessing the
viability of tunable silicon oxide (SiOx) and titanium oxide (TiOx) overlayers to impart
selective reactivity for the desired HER, over undesired reduction of the aqueous Fe(III)
or IO3– redox mediator, and the desired OER, over undesired oxidation of the aqueous
Fe(II) or I– redox mediator. Well-defined planar ultrathin-oxide-coated platinum
electrodes, for HER selectivity studies, or iridium electrodes, for OER electivity studies
were used to quantitatively evaluate key coating properties and system parameters that
affect selective transport of redox species through the coatings and redox reactivity at
metal surfaces. Planar samples were chosen due to their ease of synthesis and uniform
overlayer thickness that allows for reliable determination of transport properties.
Synthesis of SiOx-encapsulated and TiOx-encapsulated thin-film electrodes. SiOx and
TiOx overlayers with uniform thicknesses of 2 – 10 nm were deposited onto planar Pt or
Ir thin-film electrodes using a room-temperature spin-coating ultraviolet–ozone process
extensively used by the Columbia Team. The precursor used to fabricate SiOx overlayers
was polydimethylsiloxane (PDMS) dissolved in toluene and TiOx overlayers was titanium
isopropoxide (TTIP) dissolved in ethanol. Properties of these coatings can be easily
modified by altering the spin coating rate, which impacts the thickness of the overlayer,
and the amount of ultraviolet light exposure during the curing process, which impacts the
carbon content and porosity of the overlayer. All reported thicknesses were determined
using ellipsometry via a Cauchy metal oxide model, resulting in a calibration curve of
coating thickness as a function of precursor concentration (Figure 11a). Ellipsometry was
performed by each of the Columbia Team and the Hurst NLR EMN Node for a common
set of thin film samples and extracted values of thickness were determined to be quite
comparable, increasing confidence in the results.
To supplement (a) (b)
characterization by
ellipsometry, and to
confirm the lateral
uniformity of the
TiOx overlayers,
atomic force
microscopy (AFM)
was performed by
the Columbia Team
on a series of
samples. The root Figure 11. (a) Calibration curve of TiOx overlayer thickness on Pt measured by
 ellipsometry as a function of precursor concentration. (b) AFM image of a 2 µm
mean square (RMS) x 2 µm area for a 5 nm thick TiOx overlayer.
roughness of the
overlayers was ~1.4 nm for TiOx|Pt samples containing 5 nm thick TiOx overlayers. In
comparison, the Pt thin-film substrates had an RMS roughness of 0.3 nm while the SiOx
overlayers typically had an RMS roughness of 0.3 nm. As seen in the representative AFM
image shown in Figure 11b for a 5 nm thick TiOx overlayer, no evidence of “large” pinhole
defects or delamination was detected for TiOx overlayers of >3 nm in thickness.
A key aspect of our project is to transfer knowledge of synthesis and characterization of
selective oxide films on flat substrates by the Columbia Team to photocatalyst particles.

                                           Page 16 of 127

This requires the use of atomic layer deposition and the “stop flow” technique, which
exposes the powder sample to chemical precursors under static conditions with a
subsequent purge of the reactor, rather than continual flow of the chemical precursor over
the sample. This soaking step enables precursor molecules to diffuse to the heterogenous
surface of the particle. A stainless-steel tray is used as a powder sample holder that is
covered with a mesh, which enables gas diffusion to the sample and holds the sample in
place when exposed to vacuum conditions.
The Hurst NLR EMN Node used atomic layer deposition with sequential pulses of TiCl4
and H2O, to generate three different thicknesses of ultrathin TiOx layers on Pt|Si
electrodes, each at 150 °C or 300 °C.
Simultaneously, TiOx films were grown
on Si wafers as witness samples,
which were characterized by the
Columbia Team in order to correlate
atomic-layer-deposition growth
conditions with thin-film properties. In
addition, the Hurst NLR EMN Node
characterized TiOx thin films by X-Ray
Reflectivity (XRR) to characterize film
thickness, density, and roughness.
The more gradual slope of the line in
the XRR data for a TiOx layer
deposited at 300 °C compared to
deposition at 150 °C (Figure 12) Figure 12. X-ray reflectometry (XRR) results for TiOx thin
shows an increased film roughness. films deposited by atomic layer deposition at 150 °C and
 300 °C.
With the goal of transferring
knowledge gained from coatings on flat surfaces to coatings on particles, growth
conditions needed to be consistent. The Hurst NLR EMN Node had the capabilities to
deposit TiOx thin films on powders using atomic layer deposition, they did not have
capabilities to deposit SiOx thin films on powders. Deposition of SiOx coatings by atomic
layer deposition is desired for this project because SiOx is less electrically conductive than
TiOx and in general shows higher redox selectivity than TiOx when deposited by a spin-
coating ultraviolet–ozone process. Plasma chemistry is often used to deposit SiOx
coatings by atomic layer deposition, however this approach is not scalable or suitable for
deposition on particles. This required that the Hurst NLR EMN Node upgrade their atomic
layer deposition system to enable SiOx with the use of ozone. Because ozone is toxic,
permanent welding of the gas lines was required in addition to the installation of detection
points to monitor the system throughout the laboratory, and required connection to the
building toxic gas alarm system. Additionally, the system required software upgrading to
include automatic shutoff interlocked to the alarm system. Multiple ozone sensors were
added to the facility air intake to monitor the change in ozone concentration between the
building intake air and the tool monitoring points, which are intended to help avoid false
alarms during high ambient ozone days. Other modifications included enabling the
sequencing of three different precursors during deposition, i.e. aminopropyl-triethyl
oxysilane, water, and ozone, which is required for optimal SiOx deposition. The Hurst NLR
EMN Node worked closely with the NLR Environmental Safety team to ensure the safe

                                         Page 17 of 127

installation of
the toxic gas. A
portable ozone
monitor was
temporarily
used for initial
system testing
during
upgrading.
The upgraded
system was Figure 13. Index of refraction at 600 nm for SiOx thin films of various film
 thicknesses synthesized using atomic layer deposition at 150 °C for (a) thinner and
used by the (b) thicker layers, in comparison to that of stoichiometric SiO2 (orange horizontal
Hurst NLR line at 1.46).
EMN Node to
deposit ~50 SiOx films using various experimental parameters, including 100 °C, 150 °C,
and 200 °C. SiOx thin-film growth was not observed at 200 °C, while at 150 °C ellipsometry
revealed a slight change in the real part of the index of refraction as a function of thickness
(Figure 13). The index of refraction is related to the density of the film, suggesting the
density of the SiOx films varied slightly based on film thickness. For films ranging in
thickness between 2 nm and 8.5 nm, the index of refraction suggests that film density
increased with thickness (Figure 13b), which has been reported previously using other
precursors. While thinner films have a higher porosity, continued deposition increases the
rate of nucleation and the density. Film density is important for controlling selective
permeation of ionic species in coated photocatalytic particles, and is a property that can
be tuned by varying synthesis conditions, such as precursor dosing times, reactor and
precursor temperatures, and purge times. These efforts provide a valuable side-by-side
comparison of the permeabilities of SiOx and TiOx overlayers deposited by wet chemical
methods by the Columbia Team to those deposited by atomic layer deposition via the
Hurst NLR EMN Node.

and roughness. Films were

Selectivity for H2 evolution and O2 evolution using oxide-encapsulated thin-film

selectivity toward the HER for SiOx|Pt and TiOx|Pt electrodes. The permeability for each
species is defined as the product of its effective diffusion coefficient and its solubility
parameter equilibrium constant.

provided in Figure 16 for a bare Pt control (Figure 16a) and a 5 nm SiOx|Pt electrode
(Figure 16b). The dashed linear sweep voltammograms were recorded in the absence
of any Fe(II) or Fe(III) in the supporting electrolyte such that the only significant current
observed is that associated with the HER at potentials <0.0 V vs RHE. The current density
due to Fe(III) reduction on each of the electrodes can be seen as the difference between
the solid and dashed linear sweep voltammograms. On bare Pt, there is a large limiting
current density of approximately -4 mA cm-2 that is seen across a wide range of potentials
and is associated with diffusion of Fe(III) across the boundary layer (Figure 16a). This
value, along with the known bulk diffusion coefficient for Fe(III), was used to determine
kc,Fe(III) for our experimental setup. At potentials <0.0 V vs RHE, the current density again
increases due to the HER which is occurring simultaneously with Fe(III) reduction. In
sharp contrast to the behavior seen for the bare Pt electrode, SiOx|Pt electrodes exhibit
substantial suppression of the undesirable Fe(III) reduction signal across the entire range
of potentials while still showing a signal due to the HER that is very similar to that
observed with supporting electrolyte only (Figure 16b).
Linear sweep voltammograms for the 5 nm SiOx|Pt electrode in Figure 16b show a
reduction in the Fe(III) cathodic current density by >95% as compared to the bare
platinum case in Figure 16a while reducing current density in the HER region by <20%.
At -0.1 V vs RHE the SiOx|Pt electrode had a selectivity toward the HER of >92% versus
the uncoated electrode, which had only ~45% selectivity. This demonstrates the viability
of SiOx coatings to suppress undesirable reactions in the Z-Scheme system while still
allowing the desirable HER reaction to occur.
To supplement experimental results, we also performed continuum-level modeling of
species transport through thin coatings to assess the range of SiOx thicknesses, species
permeabilities, and bulk electrolyte conditions that will allow us to achieve our target
selectivities. The rate of transport of electroactive species through thin semipermeable
overlayers depends on both the properties of the overlayer (thickness (t), species
permeabilities (Pi)) as well as the properties of the bulk electrolyte (bulk concentrations
(Cb), thickness of the boundary layer (δ)). Using these parameters as inputs, we evaluated
the transport-limited current densities (ilim) for a desirable reactant (D) and undesirable
reactant (U). In the context of the H2 evolving photocatalyst particles, D represents H+
and U likely represents Fe(III). The rates of reaction of D (rD) and U (rU) can be used to
compute the reaction selectivity toward the desired species (SD/U) as follows,

for a generic species i that diffuses across the boundary layer and SiOx coating. At steady

equation gives linear concentration profiles such as that shown in Figure 17a. It follows
from Fick’s law, Faraday’s law, and the fact that diffusive flux across the overlayer must

Using Equation 6, SD/U was systematically evaluated for various overlayer characteristics
and bulk species concentrations. As an example, the transport-limited SD/U was calculated
for varying bulk concentrations of Fe(III) and H+ (expressed as pH) for a bare metal
electrode (Figure 17b) and an encapsulated electrode (Figure 17c). These figures were
generated assuming constant kc values consistent with our experiments and known
species diffusivities in the bulk electrolyte taken from literature. For the encapsulated
electrode, the coating thickness was assumed to be constant at 5 nm and species
permeabilities were those extracted from experimental measurements (PFe(III) = 5 x 10-11
cm2/s and PH+ = 1 x 10-7 cm2/s).
By comparing Figure 17b and Figure 17c, it is clear that the coated electrode is predicted
to achieve the target selectivity of SD/U > 95% over a substantially larger range of pH and
Fe(III) concentrations than can be achieved with the bare metal electrode. Additionally,
the model shows improved selectivity toward the HER at all practical system and

                                                                Page 21 of 127

overlayer parameters. Overall, the model demonstrates a wide set of operating conditions
that can be used on a coated electrode while still achieving the desired selectivity for the
HER.

partial reaction rates (rD for the HER and rU for Fe(III) reduction), which were subsequently

shows how the partial current density and selectivity toward the desirable HER vary as a
function of SiOx overlayer thickness. Increasing overlayer thickness introduces a trade-
off: larger thicknesses enable greater selectivity, but also reduce the current density
associated with the desired HER reaction. Thus, there is an optimal thickness range that
allows for high selectivity while still allowing for sufficiently high fluxes.
SiOx coatings synthesized by the standard spin-coating ultraviolet–ozone process
developed by the Columbia Team have been primarily studied when deposited onto two-
dimensional planar substrates, where coatings are often assumed to be continuous and
homogenous in their properties. However, we had previously postulated that “pinholes”
or other defects like cracks or particle protrusions through the oxide coating may facilitate
locally high rates of undesirable reactions (Figure 19). As SiOx coatings are applied to

                                               Page 22 of 127

complex particle morphologies, these coatings defects must be understood fully and their
effects characterized in terms of selectivity toward the desired redox reaction such as the
HER in photocatalytic water splitting. Local electrochemical measurements, such as
scanning electrochemical microscopy (SECM), can be used to estimate the relative rates
of production of product species generated from competing electrochemical reactions
with high spatial resolution over the electrode. As illustrated in Figure 19, the SECM
probe, AKA “tip”, is rastered over the substrate surface and used to sense electroactive
species evolved from the substrate immediately below the probe. Here, we report the use
of SECM to
determine the
influence of overlayer
defects on electrode
performance and the
computed
permeability of an
undesired redox
mediator species
(Fe(III)) through the
overlayer.

of an electrode
surface toward a
desired product,
SD,local, can be

the electrochemical

A 500 μm diameter platinum disk coated with a 10 nm thick SiOx overlayer (Figure 19a)
was used as a model substrate electrode to study the spatial differences in selectivity of
the wet chemical synthesized SiOx coating originating from defects in the overlayer. Three
separate scans were completed with constant tip potential of 1.2 V vs RHE (sufficient to
oxidize both H2 and Fe(II)) and varying substrate potentials of open-circuit potential (no
product generation), 0.1 V vs RHE (Fe(III) reduction limiting current), and -0.05 V vs RHE
(the HER and Fe(III) reduction). An example of a single line scan can be seen in Figure
19b. The chosen substrate potential limits the generation of hydrogen below rates that
would lead to bubble formation, which would interfere with the SECM measurement by
creating additional noise. A baselining procedure was used to correct for small transient
and electrolyte compositional differences between runs. A linear background was
calculated using currents where the tip was positioned over the bare silicon substrate
(position equals 0 μm and 1000 μm). Partial tip currents associated with Fe(II) oxidation
were then calculated from the difference in tip currents measured during scans completed
with substrate potentials of 0.1 V vs RHE and open-circuit potential for Fe(III) signal, while
tip current associated with H2 oxidation was calculated from the difference in tip currents
measured for substrate potentials of -0.05 V and 0.01 V, each vs RHE (see Figure 19c).
Local selectivity is then calculated through Equation 7, using the partial currents for the
desired reaction (HER) and undesired reaction (Fe(III) reduction). Selectivity can then be
plotted as a function of probe position throughout the line scan, as shown in Figure 19d.
This methodology was applied to multiple line scans across the SiOx-encapsulated Pt
disk to produce the two-dimensional selectivity map shown in Figure 20a. It can be seen
that a highly localized area of low selectivity is correlated with a particle defect in the
coating, corresponding to the highlighted feature in Figure 20b. A higher magnification
SEM image of this area, provided in Figure 20c, displays a break in the platinum
electroactive feature in addition to a particle defect, which could possibly protrude through

is organic in nature, suggests that this feature is not a platinum particle protruding through
the SiOx coating.

diffusion model of a point electrode under steady-state conditions. This relationship
correlates the current to the number of electrons per reaction, n, the diffusion coefficient
of Fe(III), DFe(III), the concentration of Fe(III) in the bulk solution, CFe(III), and the radius of

In Table 2, PFe(III) calculated according to Equation 9 is compared against the value of
PFe(III) calculated assuming that the SiOx coating is defect free and possesses completely
uniform properties at all points on the sample. Importantly, accounting for the defect
results in a two-order-magnitude decrease in the value of PFe(III). This simple calculation
is a powerful demonstration that the Fe(III) reduction reaction may occur almost entirely
through coating defects rather than through the SiOx bulk and requires local
measurements, with important implications for the performance of encapsulated
particulate photocatalysts.

Identical experiments as for SiOx|Pt were performed using TiOx coatings deposited using
a room-temperature spin-coating ultraviolet–ozone process developed by the Columbia
Team. Limiting current densities were obtained by measuring linear sweep

control electrode (Figure 21a) and a 5 nm TiOx|Pt electrode (Figure 21b). The dashed
linear sweep voltammograms were recorded in the absence of any Fe(II) or Fe(III) in the

for our experimental setup. At potentials <0.0 V vs RHE, the current density again
increases due to the HER which is occurring simultaneously with Fe(III) reduction. In
sharp contrast to the behavior seen for the bare Pt electrode, TiOx|Pt electrodes exhibit
substantial suppression of the undesirable Fe(III) reduction signal across the entire range
of potentials while still showing a signal due to the HER that is very similar to that
observed with supporting electrolyte only (Figure 21b).
Linear sweep voltammograms for the 5 nm TiOx|Pt electrode in Figure 21b show a
reduction in the Fe(III) cathodic current density by >95% as compared to the bare
platinum case in Figure 21a while reducing current density in the HER region by <20%.
At -0.1 V vs RHE, the 8 nm and 10 nm TiOx|Pt electrodes had selectivities toward the
HER of >98% versus the bare Pt electrode, which only had ~65% selectivity. This
demonstrates the viability of these ultrathin coatings to suppress undesirable reactions in
photocatalyst particle suspension reactors while still allowing the desirable HER reaction
to occur.
Linear sweep voltammograms like those shown in Figure 21 were also carried out for
TiOx|Pt electrodes with TiOx overlayers of varied thickness (i.e. t = 2.5 nm, 4 nm, 8 nm,
10 nm). Current densities recorded in the supporting electrolyte-only and the Fe(III)/Fe(II)-
containing solutions were used to estimate the experimental selectivity toward the HER
at potentials <0.0 vs RHE, just like for the SiOx|Pt electrodes. Values of SD/U over the
potential range of interest are shown in Figure 22a for each TiOx-coated electrode. This
shows that the TiOx overlayers substantially improve the selectivity toward the desired
HER, especially at the lowest HER overpotentials. Samples with 8 and 10 nm TiOx
overlayer each achieve >98% selectivity with only a 0.1 V overpotential vs the onset of
the HER, while the bare Pt electrode only achieves ~65% selectivity at the same
overpotential. Figure 22b shows how the partial current density and selectivity toward the
desired HER vary as a function of TiOx overlayer thickness. As was the case for SiOx
coatings, larger thicknesses enable greater selectivity, but also reduce the current density

                                            Page 26 of 127

associated with the desired HER reaction. In total, there is an optimal thickness range for
the ultrathin oxide coatings that allows for high selectivity while still allowing for sufficiently
high fluxes.
In comparing the performance of TiOx|Pt electrodes to SiOx|Pt electrodes, we find that
SiOx and TiOx overlayers have similar trends of decreasing species permeabilities as
thickness increases. However, there are also differences. For larger TiOx thicknesses (10

lower than for TiOx overlayers for smaller overlayer thicknesses (<5 nm). Furthermore,
there are differences in the relative permeabilities of the Fe(III) and Fe(II) species. While
the Fe(II) permeability for a given SiOx overlayer is approximately 15 – 20% greater than
the Fe(III) permeability, the opposite trend is observed for TiOx overlayers. Lastly, we note
that the SiOx overlayers generally have higher selectivities for the HER than TiOx
overlayers at thickness <5 nm but lower selectivities for the HER at thicknesses >8 nm.
We observed that TiOx-encapsulated Pt thin-film electrodes having TiOx overlayers with
thicknesses <3.5 nm exhibited electrochemical activity toward the Fe(III)/Fe(II) redox
mediator that was very similar to that observed for an unencapsulated Pt electrode. We
surmised that this might in part be due to higher electrical conductance of the thinner
overlayers and/or pinholes, which atomic layer deposited overlayers may not have.
Therefore, we compared electrochemical properties of thin-film Pt electrodes with TiOx
overlayers of varying thicknesses between 2 and 10 nm that were either deposited via
the spin-coating ultraviolet–ozone process by the Columbia Team or gas-phase atomic
layer deposition by the Hurst NLR EMN Node. Cyclic voltammetry was performed on
these electrodes between potentials of 0.42 V and 1.12 V, each vs RHE, for 4 cycles with
a scan rate of 10 mV/s. The aqueous electrolyte used for these measurements contained
25 mM FeSO4∙7H2O, 12.5 mM Fe2(SO4)3∙5H2O, and 0.1 M Na2SO4 with the pH adjusted
to 1.5 and a small 1 cm stir bar operating at 200 rpm and identical electrolyte without iron
sulfates present. From these measurements mass transfer limiting current densities for
the reduction of Fe(III) to Fe(II) were calculated by taking the difference between the

                                            Page 27 of 127

average current density measured between 0.42 V and 0.52 V, each vs RHE, in the iron-
containing and iron-free electrolytes.

 Figure 23. (a) Plot of Fe(III) reduction limiting current density as a function of TiOx thickness. Ultraviolet–
 ozone TiOx|Pt electrodes are represented by blue circles, atomic-layer-deposited TiOx|Pt by orange
 triangles, Pt|TiOx|Pt electrodes represented by green squares, and bare Pt by the horizontal black
 dashed line. (b) Diagram of Pt|TiOx|Pt electrode. Cyclic voltammograms were performed between
 potentials of 0.42 V and 1.12 V, each vs RHE, for 4 cycles with a scan rate of 10 mV/s. Electrolytes
 used were aqueous 25 mM FeSO4∙7H2O, 12.5 mM Fe2(SO4)3∙5H2O, and 0.1 M Na2SO4 with the pH

The resulting Fe(III) reduction limiting current densities are shown in Figure 23a as a
function of the TiOx thickness, which was determined by ellipsometry. For both the
ultraviolet–ozone-process TiOx|Pt electrodes and atomic-layer-deposited TiOx|Pt
electrodes with overlayer thicknesses <3.8 nm, the Fe(III) reduction limiting current
density is between -1.8 mA cm-2 and -2.2 mA cm-2, similar to that of the unencapsulated
Pt electrode. For thicknesses between 3.8 nm and 5.8 nm, the ultraviolet–ozone-process
TiOx|Pt electrodes exhibit a sharp drop in magnitude of the Fe(III) reduction limiting
current density as TiOx thickness increases, decreasing from ~2 mA cm-2 to ~0.1 mA cm-
2
  . Only one atomic-layer-deposited TiOx|Pt electrode was available with a thickness
between 3.8 nm and 5.8 nm, however, the measured limiting current density was
consistent with the trend observed with the ultraviolet-ozone-process TiOx|Pt electrodes.
At thicknesses >5.8 nm for both the ultraviolet–ozone-process TiOx|Pt and atomic-layer-
deposited TiOx|Pt electrodes, the magnitude of the Fe(III) reduction limiting current
density decreases further from ~0.1 mA cm-2 to ~0.01 mA cm-2. Based on these results it
suggests that Fe(III) limiting currents are not very sensitive to the overlayer fabrication
method, and therefore composition, density, crystallinity, and/or roughness, but are
critically dependent on the overlayer thickness.
There are at two leading hypotheses for why thicker (>4 nm) TiOx coatings attenuate
Fe(III) reduction limiting current but the thinner coatings do not:
(i) All Fe(III) reduction occurs at the TiOx|Pt buried interface and the thicker coatings are
able to almost completely suppress the transport of Fe(III) to the active Pt sites while the
thinnest coatings are not. This is consistent with the fact that the mass-transfer resistance
of an ionically-conducting coating is inversely proportional to the coating thickness.

                                                Page 28 of 127

(ii) The outer surface of the TiOx coatings possess sufficient catalytic activity toward the
Fe(III) reduction reaction, and as the thickness of the TiOx coating increases the transport
of electrons through the coating (i.e. the electrical conductivity of the coating) limits the
maximum rate of the reduction reaction rather than Fe(III) transport. If this hypothesis
holds true, the sigmoidal shape of the curve seen in Figure 23a is most likely explained
by electron transport via quantum mechanical tunneling. This is in contrast to the
relationship expected for conventional electronic conduction mechanisms that are known
to be characterized by ohmic behavior.
To test the two hypotheses described above, 1 nm of Pt was deposited on the surface of
ultraviolet–ozone-process TiOx|Pt electrodes via electron-beam evaporation to create
Pt|TiOx|Pt electrodes, which we expect should be insufficient to form a continuous metal
film, instead preferring to form small nanoparticles that will limit the occurrence of
significant shunting behavior between the outer and inner Pt layers. A diagram of this
electrode configuration is shown in Figure 23b. The Fe(III) reduction limiting current
densities of these Pt|TiOx|Pt electrodes were then evaluated by cyclic voltammetry using
the identical conditions described above for the TiOx|Pt electrodes. Results from these
Pt|TiOx|Pt electrodes are shown in Figure 23a as green squares, revealing only a slight
increase in the magnitude of the current density compared to TiOx|Pt electrodes having
identical overlayer thickness. Importantly, the fact that these electrodes containing a
highly active Pt electrocatalyst on their outer surfaces still exhibit significant reduction in
the magnitude of the limiting current density for TiOx thicknesses >5 nm strongly suggests
that electron transport from the substrate to the outer surface is the key factor limiting the
achievable current density for Pt|TiOx|Pt electrodes with TiOx thicknesses >5 nm. Given
the very similar shape of the limiting current vs TiOx thickness curves for Pt|TiOx|Pt and
TiOx|Pt electrodes, we find it likely that electron transport to active Ti centers at the outer
surface of TiOx|Pt electrodes is also limiting the Fe(III) reduction current for these
electrodes. Significantly, these results suggest it is important that TiOx overlayers
deposited on H2 evolving particles be >5 nm if the overlayers are going to sufficiently
attenuate the undesired Fe(III) reduction reaction. For <0.1 mA cm-2 current densities, the
TiOx coating should be even thicker to enable this blocking behavior. Conversely, if it is
desirable for TiOx coatings to allow Fe(III) reduction on their outer surface (e.g. for O2
evolving particles), coatings should be <5 nm thick.
The Columbia Team also developed a second method to verify electrode selectivities. All
our previous measurements calculated selectivity toward the desired HER or OER
reaction by analyzing linear sweep voltammograms. Using this method, the selectivity of
an electrode towards the desirable reaction (SD/U) was calculated as follows,
 𝑖𝐷
 𝑟𝐷 𝑛𝐷 𝐹 𝑖𝐷 𝑖𝑡𝑜𝑡 − 𝑖𝑙𝑖𝑚,𝑈

 𝑟𝐷 + 𝑟𝑈 + 𝑈 𝑖𝐷 + 𝑛 𝐷 𝑖𝑈 𝑖𝑡𝑜𝑡 − (1 − 𝑛𝐷 )𝑖𝑙𝑖𝑚,𝑈

where rj is the rate of reaction j, itot is the total current density, ij is the partial current
density towards reaction j, ilim,j is the limiting current density, nj is the stochiometric number
of electrons, and F is the Faraday constant. Subscript D stands for the desired reactions,
i.e. HER or OER, while U stands for the undesired mediator back reaction. A
consequence of defining selectivity according to Equation 10 based on analysis of linear
sweep voltammograms is that the calculated value of SD/U only applies to the brief period

                                                  Page 29 of 127

of time while the linear sweep voltammogram is measured, which is typically ~1 min. In
contrast, photocatalysis measurements are often carried out over many hours. Hence,
we herein refer to selectivities calculated from analysis of linear sweep voltammograms
as instantaneous selectivities.
To compare
instantaneous
selectivities to
time-averaged
selectivities, we
developed a
second
experimental
method and
setup, which is
illustrated in
Figure 24. The
cell used in this
setup is divided
into a primary
 Figure 24. Schematic of setup used for measuring time-averaged selectivities,
chamber, where ̅ , for oxide-encapsulated electrodes.
 𝑆𝐷/𝑈
the working
electrode (WE) is
inserted along with the reference electrode (RE), and a secondary chamber that contains
the counter electrode (CE). The two chambers are divided by an ion-exchange membrane
to minimize crossover of mediator species between the WE and CE while still allowing for
pH balancing and ion transport between them. Initially, both chambers are filled with
supporting electrolyte but only the main chamber contains the mediator molecules of
interest, i.e. Fe(II) and Fe(III). After inserting electrodes, the experiment is initiated by
performing a potentiostatic chronoamperometry experiment for multiple hours.
Periodically, small aliquots of electrolyte are sampled from each chamber, and electronic
absorption spectroscopy is used to determine concentrations of redox mediator species.
In this work, we monitored the decrease in Fe(III) concentration by tracking the decrease
in absorbance of the electrolyte at 400 nm. At this wavelength, only Fe(III) is optically
active, allowing for straightforward determination of the concentration of Fe(III) based on
the empirically-determined linear relationship between absorbance and Fe(III)
concentration for control solutions, consistent with the Beer–Bouger–Lambert law. This
relationship was found to be independent of pH for pH values <1.5.

 𝑆𝐻2/𝐹𝑒(𝐼𝐼) = 0.17. This value is significantly lower than our previously reported

 discrepancies between the instantaneous and time-averaged selectivities may result from
 one or more of the following causes: (i) build-up of H2 bubbles on the electrode surface
 during longer chronoamperometry measurements may lead to relative differences in

                                              Page 31 of 127

local pH at the WE surface during longer chronoamperometry experiments may become
slightly elevated due to the HER, lowering the effective overpotential for the HER, (iii)
minor drift in the RE potential, and/or (iv) slightly lower HER activity for the Pt electrode
used in the chronoamperometry experiment compared to that used in the linear sweep
voltammogram experiment. Notwithstanding, we think that a combination of cyclic
voltammetry experiments and coupled chronoamperometry and spectroscopy
experiments provide important complementary information relevant to understanding
coating selectivities of importance to photocatalysts.
To provide additional insight into the chemical origins of the trends in species
permeabilities and redox selectivities for the HER over Fe(III) reduction measured
experimentally by the Columbia Team, and to develop more general design rules for the
overlayers, the Ogitsu LLNL EMN Node performed atomistic simulations on related model
systems. They simulated the transport of water, Fe(II), and Fe(III) through ultrathin SiO2
materials containing a single pore of varied diameter. With additional information on
properties that result in desired redox reactivity, the Columbia Team and Hurst NLR EMN
Node can fine-tune coating properties by varying organometallic precursor, annealing
time, and/or annealing temperature, which each influence porosity and carbon content in
the coatings.
Potentials of mean force obtained from atomistic simulations and shown in Figure 26
indicate that water can easily transport through an SiO2 coating layer, if sub-nanometer
pores are present. This favorable transport is related to the interaction between water
molecules and silanol groups, which overcomes the energy penalty for breaking water
hydrogen bonding to enter the pore. In sharp contrast to water, transport mechanisms for
Fe(III) and Fe(II) in the coating layer are much more complex. Results indicate that ions
experience high barriers of >20 kJ/mol to enter the pore (Figure 26, black line, upper
panel), suggesting that transport of the ion
to the underneath interface is rather
limited. We also carried out simulations for
a wider SiO2 pore and determined that the
barrier for ion transport into the pore
remains positive (Figure 26, red line,
upper panel) and is larger than that of
water. These data indicate that the
permeability of Fe(II) (and Fe(III); data not
shown) is much smaller than that of water,
an observation that is consistent with
experimental results. Collectively,
simulation results support the conclusion
that SiOx coatings can greatly attenuate
Fe(III) reduction due to a large kinetic
energy barrier for the ion to access the
underneath interface, while still allowing
water to transport and participate in the Figure 26. Two-dimensional potential of mean force
 (PMF) free energy profiles of water and Fe(II)
HER. transport through an SiO2 slit pore with a width of 0.95
 and 1.35 nm (measured by Si–Si distance) (top), and
 a snapshot of the simulation model (bottom).

                                           Page 32 of 127

To better understand the origin of the low permeability of Fe(II) in the SiO2 coating, we
analyzed in more detail the structure of the first and second solvation shells of the Fe(II)
in the bulk solution and within the 0.95 nm pore. We found that Fe(II) retains the same
number of water molecules in the first solvation shell, while exhibiting the loss of about
three water molecules from the second shell (Figure 27a). In addition, analysis of the O–
Fe(II)–O angular distribution for water within the first and second shells (Figure 27b,c)
shows that these solvation shells are distorted as compared to those in the bulk solution.
Accordingly, our results suggest that a
high energy penalty for ion transport into
the pore can be attributed to the (a)
distortion of the first and second
solvation shells, as well as the partial
dehydration of the second solvation
shell of the ion.
Collectively, these simulation results
support the experimental observation
that SiOx overlayers greatly attenuate
currents due to Fe(III) reduction while (b) (c)
still allowing water transport and the
HER.

electrodes with a scan rate of 20 mV/s for 4 cycles to varying vertex potentials such that
each of the electrodes would remain stable in solution. A representative cyclic
voltammogram for each of the electrodes is shown in Figure 28a, and it was observed
that all three electrodes exhibited similar activity for I– oxidation with initial onset beginning
at ~1.2 V vs RHE. Additionally, it was observed for IO3– reduction that the platinum
electrode (black) had the earliest onset potential occurring at 0.4 V vs RHE compared to
0.05 V vs RHE for the gold electrode (orange). Mass-transport-limited currents for IO3–
reduction are achieved for both the gold and platinum electrodes with a current density of
approximately -6.5 mA cm-2. The glassy carbon electrode (blue), however, does not
exhibit facile kinetics for IO3– reduction and fails to reach a mass-transport-limited current.
These results are significant because they demonstrate that electrocatalyst choice can
have a significant impact on IO3–/I– redox activity and do so in a way that is highly
asymmetric, where oxidation is more kinetically facile than reduction. This has major

                                            Page 33 of 127

To evaluate the effect of coatings, cyclic voltammetry was performed on bare
unencapsulated Pt and Pt electrodes encapsulated by 10 nm thick SiOx overlayers,
deposited using the standard spin-coating ultraviolet–ozone process developed by the
Columbia Team, at a scan rate of 20 mV/s for 4 cycles between potentials of -0.1 V and
1.75 V, each vs RHE. The electrolyte used was aqueous 5 mM NaI, 5 mM NaIO3, and 0.1
M Na SO with the pH adjusted to 10 and identical electrolyte without the presence of
 2 4

iodine. Representative cyclic voltammograms are shown in Figure 28b,c for the bare Pt
and 10 nm SiOx|Pt electrodes, respectively, where solid lines represent cyclic

                                            Page 34 of 127

voltammograms performed with electrolyte containing iodine species and dashed lines
represent cyclic voltammograms without iodine species present. The HER activity for the
electrodes can be observed in the dashed line cyclic voltammograms at potentials <0.0
V vs RHE. This HER activity reaches a maximum cathodic current density at -0.1 V vs
RHE of approximately -1.5 mA cm-2 and -0.3 mA cm-2 for the bare Pt and 10 nm SiOx|Pt
electrodes, respectively. These current densities are significantly lower than those
previously reported for the HER on Pt electrodes in pH 1.5 aqueous electrolyte because
platinum-based electrodes have slower kinetics for the HER in alkaline solutions. The
current density associated with IO3– reduction can be observed as the difference between
the solid and dashed lines in Figure 28b,c. Here it is observed that IO3– reduction onset
occurs at 0.4 V vs RHE and reaches a limiting current density of approximately -6.5 mA
cm-2 while the encapsulated electrode does not display IO3– reduction until approximately
0.2 V vs RHE and reaches a current of approximately -0.1 mA cm-2 at 0.05 V vs RHE.
These results demonstrate that SiOx overlayers can greatly suppress IO3– reduction on
platinum electrodes while still allowing for moderate HER activity. Figure 28d shows the
selectivity toward the HER over IO3– reduction as a function of overpotential. These
selectivity values were calculated using the method described above, with the IO3–
reduction current densities linearly extrapolated from those measured at 0.05 – 0.15 V vs
RHE to more negative potentials. Here it is observed that the platinum electrode (black
line) has a maximum selectivity value of <0.2 at current densities between -5 mA cm-2
and -10 mA cm-2 while the encapsulated electrode (green line) exhibits significantly
enhanced selectivity between current densities of -0.1 and -0.3 mA cm-2 and ultimately
achieves a maximum selectivity of 0.95. This important result is even more significant
because the kinetics of the HER are slower at these higher pH values, meaning that
coatings must be even more selective for the HER over reduction of the oxidized form of
the redox mediator than at more acidic pH values.
Many photocatalysis experiments use sacrificial electron donors to isolate processes due
to photogenerated electrons. A common sacrificial electron donor is methanol. To better
understand how ultrathin oxide coatings affect methanol oxidation, both the HER and
methanol oxidation behavior were qualitatively evaluated using thin-film Pt electrodes
encapsulated by 5 – 10 nm thick SiOx and TiOx overlayers deposited using the standard
photochemical deposition procedure utilized by the Columbia Team. To evaluate the
influence of pH and methanol concentration, three different electrolyte conditions were
evaluated, which resemble those commonly used in photocatalyst studies, as well as to
provide a comparison to previous work reported by the Columbia Team for methanol
oxidation through SiOx overlayers.5 The three electrolytes used for this study were:
Solution #1 (pH 7, 10 % v/v CH3OH + 0.5 M Na2SO4), Solution #2 (10% v/v CH3OH +
0.05 M H2SO4 + 0.45 M Na2SO4 at pH 1.5), Solution #3 (pH 0.66, 0.5 M CH3OH + 0.5 M
H2SO4).5 For each sample and solution, three sets of cyclic voltammetry measurements
were performed. First, the methanol oxidation activity was evaluated by sweeping the
potential between +0.2 V and +1.3 V, each vs RHE, for 10 sweeps at 100 mV/s,
whereupon the behavior reached a steady state. HER behavior was then evaluated using
cyclic voltammetry measurements conducted over a more negative potential range of
approximately -0.3 – 0.1 V vs RHE for 10 sweeps, with the lower potential limit altered
depending on the electrolyte conditions to ensure that the HER occurs without excess
influence of H2 bubbles. The third and final set of cyclic voltammetry measurements were

                                      Page 35 of 127

carried out over the full potential range of -0.3 – 1.3 V vs RHE in order to observe the
HER and methanol oxidation features simultaneously. The final sweep from the third set
of cyclic voltammograms is shown in Figure 29 for all samples and solutions, where
dashed vertical lines have been included to indicate the onset potential of each reaction.

 Figure 29. Final full window cyclic voltammetry cycle for planar platinum electrodes with ~10 nm thick
 SiOx or TiOx overlayer coatings in deaerated (a) Solution #1 (pH 7, aqueous 10% v/v methanol + 0.5 M
 Na2SO4), (b) Solution #2 (pH 1.5, aqueous 10% methanol v/v + 0.05 M H2SO4 + 0.45 M Na2SO4), and
 (c) Solution #3 (pH 0.66, aqueous 0.5 M methanol + 0.5 M H2SO4). Scans were conducted at room
 temperature using a scan rate of 100 mV/s.

A first key observation from Figure 29 is that the 10 nm thick SiOx and TiOx coatings do
not impede the ability of Pt electrodes to carry out methanol oxidation; in fact, one or both
of the oxide coatings are observed to enhance methanol oxidation current densities at
positive potentials for all three solutions. The enhancement in methanol oxidation for
SiOx|Pt in Solution 3 is consistent with that previously reported by the Columbia Team.
With varying overlayer composition and electrolyte conditions there are notable
differences in the shape and location of peaks due to methanol oxidation in the cyclic
voltammograms, but identifying the cause of these effects are beyond the scope of this
project. A second key observation from Figure 29 is that the HER activity of the oxide-
encapsulated electrodes increases as the pH of the electrolyte is lowered. Under the most
acidic conditions studied (Solution #3, Figure 29c) almost no notable difference between
the HER traces of the coated electrodes is seen relative to the bare Pt control. However,
the HER overpotentials for the coated electrodes clearly increase relative to bare Pt at
the intermediate pH (Solution #2, Figure 29b), and these differences are amplified even
further when the pH-neutral electrolyte is used (Solution #1, Figure 29a). Notably, the
negative shift in the HER onset potential to around -0.2 V vs RHE for Solution #1 can be
attributed to a mechanistic shift in the HER from proton reduction to water reduction. Thus,
we attribute differences in HER performance of the coated and bare Pt samples to the
ability of the oxide overlayers to easily facilitate the HER via H+ transport through the
overlayer in acidic solutions, while the overlayer suppresses H2O and/or OH– transport in
the pH-neutral electrolyte. Importantly, Figure 29 shows that the 10 nm thick SiOx and
TiOx coatings do not adversely affect the ability of a buried Pt electrocatalyst to oxidize
methanol under any of the tested conditions, but they can require additional overpotential
for the HER in pH-neutral solutions.
We also report the cyclic voltammograms presented in Figure 29 because they can be
used to estimate the minimum photovoltage that a photocatalyst particle must generate
to carry out coupled methanol oxidation and the HER at sufficient steady-state currents.

than in photocatalytic measurements due to comparing a dynamic voltammetry
measurement to a presumed constant-voltage photocatalysis experiment and differences
in the number and type of sites for methanol oxidation and the HER in an actual composite
photocatalyst particle. Here, we assumed that both the HER and methanol oxidation were
driven at overpotential-based currents consistent with those at Pt, and that there was an
equal number of sites for both reactions. However, for actual photocatalyst particles,
methanol oxidation may occur at the semiconductor|liquid junction and thus could have
significantly more active sites than the metal cocatalyst based on typical cocatalyst
loadings.
In total, we showed that the HER and methanol oxidation occurred to a similar degree
when Pt thin-film electrodes were coated in a 2 – 10 nm ultrathin SiOx or TiOx overlayer
deposited by a spin-coating ultraviolet–ozone process or gas-phase atomic layer
deposition, but that Fe(III/II) redox and IO3–/I– redox can be selectively attenuated.
Given the ability to selectively drive the HER and methanol oxidation over redox mediator
reactions, we performed similar analyses for the OER using Ir thin-film electrodes
containing SiOx and TiOx overlayers of uniform thicknesses of 2 – 10 nm deposited using
the same room-temperature spin-coating ultraviolet–ozone process extensively used by
the Columbia Team to deposit SiOx layers. Reported thicknesses are based on calibration
curves for overlayer thickness versus precursor concentration for overlayers deposited
on smooth Pt electrodes under identical conditions. These approximate thicknesses were
used as we were unable to determine thicknesses using ellipsometry due to the rougher
nature of the FTO substrate on which Ir was deposited.
Limiting current densities for the OER and Fe(II) oxidation reactions were obtained by
measuring linear sweep voltammograms at 20 mV/s between potentials of 0.42 V and
1.65 V, each vs RHE, with representative data shown in Figure 31. The current density
due to Fe(II) oxidation on each of the electrodes can be approximated as the difference

                                       Page 37 of 127

Figure 31. Linear sweep voltammograms for (a) a planar bare Ir electrode, (b) a 10 nm SiOx|Ir electrode,
and (c) a 10 nm TiOx|Ir electrode with (solid curve) and without (dashed curve) the Fe(III)/Fe(II) redox
couple. The electrode was cured using the ultraviolet–ozone treatment for 120 minutes. Electrolyte: aq 25
mM FeSO4∙7H2O, 12.5 mM Fe2(SO4)3∙5H2O, and 0.1 M Na2SO4 with the pH adjusted to 1.5.

between the data with (solid curve) and without (dashed curve) Fe(III)/Fe(II) present. In
the Fe(II)/Fe(III)-containing electrolyte, the linear sweep voltammograms for bare Ir
electrodes exhibit a sharp increase in oxidation current around the reversible potential for
the Fe(II)/Fe(III) couple before reaching a mass-transfer-limited current density of ~2.5
mA cm-2 between ~1.0 V and ~1.4 V, each vs NHE. For these bare samples, this limiting
current is associated with the maximum rate of diffusion of Fe(II) across the boundary
layer (Figure 31a). This value, along with the known bulk diffusion coefficient for Fe(II),
was used to determine the mass-transfer coefficient for this species, kc,Fe(II), for our
experimental setup. At potentials more positive than 1.55 V vs RHE the current density
increases again due to the onset of the OER that occurs simultaneously with Fe(II)
oxidation.
In sharp contrast to the behavior observed for the bare Ir electrodes, SiOx|Ir and TiOx|Ir
electrodes exhibit substantial suppression of the undesirable Fe(II) oxidation signal
across the entire range of potentials while still showing a sharp increase in current starting
at ~1.5 V vs RHE that is very similar to that observed for the OER for electrodes immersed
in redox-inert aqueous Na2SO4 supporting electrolyte only (Figure 31b,c). Linear sweep
voltammograms for the 10 nm SiOx|Ir and TiOx|Ir electrodes show a decrease in the
current density associated with Fe(II) oxidation by >90% for potentials <1.4 V vs RHE as
compared to the case of the bare Ir electrode (Figure 31a) while maintaining or slightly
increasing current density in the OER region. At 1.65 V vs RHE the 5 nm SiOx|Ir and
TiOx|Ir electrodes had selectivities toward the OER of >98%, while the uncoated Ir
electrode only had a selectivity of ~65% toward the OER at the same potential. This
demonstrates the viability of the coatings to suppress undesirable reactions in the Z-
Scheme particle suspension reactor design while still allowing the desirable OER to
occur.
Linear sweep voltammograms like those shown in Figure 31 were also carried out for
SiOx|Ir and TiOx|Ir electrodes with SiOx and TiOx overlayers of varied thickness (i.e. t = 2
nm, 5 nm, 10 nm). Selectivities for the OER at potentials >1.23 V vs RHE were calculated
using the method described above, with the Fe(II) oxidation current densities linearly
extrapolated from those measured at 1.1 – 1.4 V vs RHE to values >1.4 V vs RHE.
Resulting partial OER current densities were then converted into partial reaction rates (rD

                                             Page 38 of 127

for the OER and rU for Fe(II) oxidation) using Faraday’s law, which were subsequently
substituted into Equation 10 to determine SD/U selectivities over the potential range of
interest (Figure 32a,c). These data indicate that SiOx and TiOx overlayers substantially
improve the selectivity toward the desired OER, especially at larger OER overpotentials.
Notably, the 10 nm SiOx|Ir electrode achieved a selectivity toward the OER of >88% at
1.65 V vs RHE. Figure 32b,d shows how the partial current density and selectivity toward
the desirable OER vary as a function of SiOx and TiOx overlayer thickness. Increasing
overlayer thickness introduces a trade-off: larger thicknesses enable higher selectivity,
but also reduce the current density associated with the desired reaction. Thus, there is
an optimal thickness range that allows for high selectivity while still allowing for sufficiently
high reaction rates.
In comparing the performance of the SiOx|Ir and TiOx|Ir electrodes we find many
similarities including minimal decreases, or even slight increases, in current density

Additionally, we observe that SiOx|Ir electrodes exhibit increased selectivity toward the
