We also evaluated SiOx|Ir electrodes in electrolyte conditions containing less redox
mediator (i.e. 3 mM FeSO4∙7H2O and 1.5 mM Fe (SO4)3∙5H2O) and at a slightly lower stir
 2

rate (i.e. 120 rpm, instead of 200 rpm). This new electrolyte composition is more
consistent with concentrations likely to be used in a practical Z-Scheme reactor based on
optical and transport properties of common redox mediators, as determined by our prior
numerical modeling results, and typical optimized concentrations used in state-of-the-art
literature demonstrations, which are in general <3 mM. Additionally, we evaluated the
selectivity of our electrodes as a function of current density, which may be a more relevant
way of evaluating coating performance as it relates to practical conditions for particles in
a Z-Scheme reactor.
Based on favorable selectivities that we previously identified, we focused on 10 nm SiOx|Ir
electrodes over use of TiOx overlayers or thinner SiOx overlayers. Figure 33 shows
representative cyclic voltammetry scans performed at 20 mV/s scan rates and compared
to bare Ir electrodes, in both the absence and presence of Fe(III)/Fe(II) redox mediator.
Similar OER activity is observed for the Ir and SiOx|Ir electrodes, with the latter showing
a decrease in OER current density of ~10% at 1.75 V vs RHE with little-to-no shift in the
OER onset potential. At potentials between 1.3 V and 1.4 V, each vs RHE, where only
Fe(II) oxidation can occur, the average current density for Fe(II) oxidation is 0.18 mA/cm2
for the bare Ir electrode and 0.076 mA/cm2 for the SiOx|Ir electrode, reflecting the ability
of the SiOx overlayers to suppress Fe(II) oxidation while still permitting the desired OER.
Selectivities of the Ir and SiOx|Ir electrodes toward the OER were calculated based on
the analysis procedure described above, but with three minor updates to ensure more
accurate determination of selectivity values. First, current densities in the forward and
reverse sweeps of the cyclic voltammograms were averaged to remove non-Faradaic
contributions to the current such as the double layer capacitance; doing so becomes
important for the selectivity analysis from cyclic voltammograms when the capacitive
current approaches the same order of magnitude as the Faradaic current. Second, cyclic
voltammograms in the iron-free supporting electrolyte were translated vertically such that
the current density at 1.4 V vs RHE, corresponding to the positive-most potential used for
evaluating Fe(II) oxidation, is zero. Subsequently, the cyclic voltammograms for the same
electrode measured in the iron-containing electrolyte were shifted by the same amount;
this correction establishes a zero-current-density baseline in the supporting electrolyte.
Third, instead of setting the Fe(II) oxidation current density as a constant value based on
the average current density between 1.3 V and 1.4 V, each vs RHE, a linear fit was
applied over the same potential window and extrapolated to positive potentials where
both Fe(II) oxidation and the OER occur; this always resulted in a larger extrapolated
current density for Fe(II) oxidation, and thus smaller, and more conservative, selectivity
values than those calculated using the previous method.
Figure 34 shows calculated selectivities toward the OER over Fe(II) oxidation as a
function of overpotential, as well as a function of current density. All samples exhibit a
sharp increase in selectivity at an overpotential of around 0.2 V, corresponding to the

Oxide encapsulation can selectively attenuate transport of undesired reactants from
reaching underlying electrocatalytic active sites, thereby creating different
microenvironments at the buried interface between the cocatalyst and overlayer where
species concentrations can vary greatly from bulk electrolyte conditions. We
hypothesized that the different chemical and/or electronic environment experienced at
buried sites versus exposed sites can influence charge-carrier separation, even in the
absence of built-in solid-state Schottky junctions or pn-junctions as is commonly believed
to be necessary for electronic carrier collection in conventional photocatalyst particles.
Elucidation of these local effects necessitates the use of local probe measurements, such
as SECM and scanning photocurrent microscopy (SPCM). SECM uses nanoscale or
microscale sensor electrodes called ultramicroelectrodes (UMEs) positioned close to the
location of interest to electrochemically detect redox species in situ and reveal local
reactions rates and selectivity. SPCM typically uses a focused laser beam to locally
generate electrons and holes, while measuring the photocurrent that results from local
illumination. By combining these techniques and designing a model planar system to
mimic a cocatalyst-decorated photocatalyst particle, we aimed to (i) glean insights into
the role(s) that coatings have on local reaction rates and carrier collection in
heterogeneous photo-active samples with more than one active site and (ii) develop
methodological protocols for SECM and SPCM on these larger scale substrates that can
then be translated for use with single particles.
The model planar system studied by the Columbia Team to explore the role of oxide
encapsulation on separation of electron/hole pairs is shown in Figure 35. This system
had larger cocatalyst features (i.e. 10 – 100 μm wide fingers), allowing for the use of
physical vapor deposition techniques to deposit desired patterns of cocatalyst features
through shadow masks. Additionally, spatial variation between features was introduced

a significant increase in the negative (i.e.

While Pt encapsulated with ~10 nm SiOx severely limits any Fe(II)/Fe(III)
electrochemistry, the sample was used previously in the workup and is likely to have
damaged the blocking properties. It is therefore likely that the positive increase is
associated with a reduction in Fe(III) concentration, via Fe(III) reduction at the substrate.
Additional line scans completed around the Fe(II)/Fe(III) reversible potential (i.e. ~0.7 V
vs RHE) confirms this behavior is not likely due to H2 electrochemistry. These p-Si
photoelectrodes do display very small built-in barriers and very weak rectification
behavior, as seen in current–voltage curves in the absence of electrolyte, which may play
a role in this observed phenomenon. However, these solid-state junctions cannot fully
explain the full behavior, as they contain symmetric Pt|Ti|p-Si contacts and do not display
the same phenomena on fully exposed samples.
While the origin of the observations in Figure 37 need to be confirmed through further
experimentation, these preliminary results (i) demonstrate that SECM can be used to
locally detect oxidation/reduction signal from neighboring cocatalyst sites on an unbiased
photoactive sample, and (ii) may provide the evidence that partial encapsulation of a
cocatalyst could be used to enhance charge separation in photocatalytic particles. These
measurements also demonstrate the usefulness of the planar photocatalyst particle
analog platform to gain insights into the underlying influences of oxide encapsulation

                                        Page 44 of 127

layers, not only on selective electrocatalysis but also phenomena like electronic charge-
carrier collection.

Once these protocols and data analysis methods were more fully developed, we planned
to transition to evaluating Ir-doped SrTiO3 particles and possibly other newly discovered
photocatalyst particle materials.
H2 evolution from suspensions of oxide-encapsulated Ir-doped SrTiO3 photocatalyst
particles. We observed that Rh-doped SrTiO3 particles exhibited superior photocatalytic
activity to Ir-doped SrTiO3 particles (Figure 38), even though Rh-doped SrTiO3 was
present at half the concentration of Ir-doped SrTiO3. Using the absorption coefficient
spectrum for a Rh-doped SrTiO3 thin film deposited by pulsed-laser deposition (Appl.

                                           Page 45 of 127

Phys. Lett., 2012, 101, 033910), we simulated the absorbed photon flux spectrum of each
particle in a 1,000 particle-deep system under 1 Sun solar illumination conditions,
assuming an approximate average particle diameter of 300 nm. These results suggest
that particles located deeper in the reactor will absorb more red-shifted lower energy light
that those near the front of the reactor. These calculations also suggest that the “1 Sun
condition” in Figure 38 is <40 mW, which is the power of the laser light source one should
use to achieve the same absorbed photon flux as when the cuvette reactor is illuminated
by 1 Sun of AM1.5G sunlight.

 (a) (b)

 Figure 39. (a) Partial pressure for m/z = 2 (H2; black) and m/z = 18 (water; green) in reactor headspace
 above an aqueous suspension (10% v/v methanol) of Rh-doped SrTiO3 particles (0.5 mg/mL)
 containing Pt cocatalysts as a function of reaction time and incident 405 nm laser irradiance used (red;
 right axis). (b) Corresponding photocatalytic performance reported as external quantum yield as a
 function of incident laser irradiance.

In all experiments we observe a trend that EQY decreases as the incident 405 nm laser
irradiance increases. During a typical experiment, light is on for 90 min followed by being
off for 20 min, and then this procedure is repeated an additional time, followed by
increasing the laser irradiance and repeating, for a total of 6 different irradiances in the
range of ~5 mW to ~140 mW, i.e. 12 light on-off events in total with, for instance, 10, 10,
28, 28, 48, 48, 87, 87, 105, 105, 131,131 mW laser irradiance. If sample aging occurs,
such a laser irradiance order will make deconvolution of those effects difficult. To
elucidate the reasoning behind this trend, aqueous suspensions of Rh-doped SrTiO3
particles were illuminated in a different laser irradiance order, with representative results
shown as Figure 39. As shown in Figure 39a, the partial pressure of H2 (black trace, m/z
= 2) is reported as a function of reaction time, with associated laser irradiance used
reported as the red trace. Unlike previous experiments, the laser irradiance was first
decreased and subsequently increased. The calculated EQYs are reported in Figure 39b,
with the order of light on–off events indicated as the red arrows. Notably, due to the
mechanical energy introduced by the stir bar and local heating caused by absorption of
laser light, water molecules can vaporize from the liquid phase into the gas phase,
causing sporadic spikes in the signal for m/z = 2. Thus, for all EQY calculations, partial
pressures for m/z = 2 (H2) were interpreted when partial pressures for m/z = 18 (water)
were stable, to mitigate confounding interpretations of ionization of water resulting in
erroneous m/z = 2 signals. A small amount of hysteresis is observed in the EQY data,
with the second round of light on–off events resulting in a slightly lower EQY. Such

                                             Page 46 of 127

hysteresis could possibly be due to sample aging at high photon fluxes, observed
nanoparticle aggregation toward the meniscus of the reaction electrolyte as the reaction
proceeds, or sample-to-sample variation. Irrespective, the averaged EQY as a function
of laser irradiance still follows the general trend where the EQY decreases as laser
irradiance increases. These results suggest that under typical experimental conditions
(i.e. 24 hours of reaction time), the effect of sample aging is negligible and the previous
conclusions of selectivity changes due to ultrathin oxide coatings are likely not affected
by sample aging.
An important aspect of our project is to transfer knowledge of synthesis and
characterization of selective ultrathin oxide films deposited on flat substrates to
photocatalyst particles. Atomic layer deposition of ultrathin TiOx overlayers (10 nm) onto
Rh-doped SrTiO3 particles (ID33A and ID34A, with Pt cocatalysts) was performed by the
Hurst NLR EMN Node for assessment of photocatalytic H2 evolution activity by the Irvine
Team. This thickness was chosen because it was likely to result in large EQYs for H2
evolution, based on experimental results obtained by the Columbia Team at pH 1.5 on
well-defined planar thin-film Pt electrodes and modeling results from the Michigan Team.
Mass spectrometry results shown in Figure 38 indicate extremely small EQYs for H2
evolution when TiOx coatings are present, which are slightly improved after
photodeposition of Pt cocatalysts on the exterior of the TiOx-coated particles. These data
suggest that the TiOx coating attenuates H2 evolution activity with photogenerated
conduction-band electrons, attenuates methanol reactivity with photogenerated valence-
band holes, and/or absorbs incident ~405 nm light. The latter hypothesis was ruled out
based on spectral response measurements, which indicate that wavelengths of light that
are not absorbed by TiOx, but are absorbed by Rh-doped SrTiO3, result in no discernable
values for EQY. To assess the ability of ultrathin coatings to allow for methanol oxidation,
additional studies were performed.
As such, the Hurst NLR EMN Node performed two more depositions of thinner TiOx
overlayers (2 nm and 3 nm) by atomic layer deposition on the same batch of Rh-doped
SrTiO3 particles that already contained Pt cocatalysts. Photocatalytic performance
measured by the Irvine Team across three different TiOx coating thicknesses is
summarized in Figure 40.
Uncoated Rh-doped SrTiO3 particles containing Pt cocatalysts and in the presence of
aqueous methanol as the sacrificial electron donor exhibited a maximum EQY of 1.8 ±
0.4% (black data) at the lowest incident irradiance used (~10 mW, which if fully absorbed
would result in ~3 mA of current over a ~1 cm2 area of the face of the cuvette reactor
orthogonal to the direction of propagation of the incident laser light), and which is
comparable to the state-of-the-art EQY value reported in the literature of ~5%. When
methanol was replaced with the common state-of-the-art Fe(III)/Fe(II) redox mediator in
a 1:1 molar ratio and the pH adjusted to ~1.5, the maximum EQY was approximately
halved to 1.090 ± 0.002% (green data), again at the lowest incident photon flux used.
Irrespective of the nature of the electron donor, i.e. methanol or Fe(II), as the thickness
of the TiOx overlayer increased, EQY values decreased, i.e. ID34 > ID34B > ID34C >
ID34A, with substantially larger decreases in EQY values when Fe(III)/Fe(II) was used.
Also, in general, EQY values decreased as the incident photon flux increased, except at
higher photon fluxes where EQY values increased for the 2 nm and 3 nm thick TiOx

To further elucidate causes for the reduced EQY compared to non-coated particles,
photocatalytic performance of the same batch of Rh-doped SrTiO3 particles containing Pt
cocatalysts, and coated in either 2 nm TiOx or SiOx, were measured by the Irvine Team
across two electrolytes with different electron donor species (Figure 41). In general, EQY
values decreased as the incident photon flux increased, and the uncoated samples
perform better under the same electrolyte conditions. Uncoated Rh-doped SrTiO3
particles containing Pt cocatalysts and in the presence of aqueous methanol as the
sacrificial electron donor exhibited a maximum EQY of 2.5 ± 0.6% at the lowest incident
irradiance used (~10 mW), and which is comparable to the state-of-the-art EQY value
reported in the literature of ~5%. When methanol was replaced with the common Fe(II)
redox mediator and the pH adjusted to ~1.5, the maximum EQY decreased to 0.15 ±
0.02%, again at the lowest incident photon flux used. Irrespective of the nature of the
electron donor, i.e. methanol or Fe(II), or the composition of the coating, i.e. TiOx or SiOx,
EQY values decreased in the presence of a coating as ID6A > ID6AA ≈ ID6AB. Another
interesting observation is that the coating resulted in a more significant decrease in EQY
when Fe(II) was used, compared to when methanol was used. This further corroborates
our previous hypothesis that Rh-doped SrTiO3 particles containing Pt cocatalysts and
coated in an ultrathin TiOx overlayer (~10 nm thick) exhibited extremely small EQY values
for H2 evolution, likely due to attenuation of reactivity with photogenerated conduction-

SrTiO3 particles containing Pt cocatalysts were measured by the Irvine Team across three
different aqueous iron redox shuttle concentrations (Figure 42). In general, EQY values
decreased as the incident photon flux increased, and the uncoated samples performed

absorption by Fe(III) at the operating conditions. The coated samples are also more
sensitive to changes in Fe(III) concentration, suggesting that the TiOx coatings might
disturb the formation of iron oxide/hydroxide complexes on the particle surfaces, which is
a complex previously reported to be beneficial for inhibiting back reactions, like H2
oxidation, from occurring on the particle surface.

because since the metal oxide surface has more hydrophilic groups than the metallic Pt

beneficial impact of coatings on EQYs for H2 evolution from doped SrTiO3 particles.
To explain the above rationalization in greater detail, the Columbia Team has shown that
introduction of ultrathin oxide coatings on platinum planar electrodes significantly
suppress both the Fe(II) oxidation reaction (FeOR) and the Fe(III) reduction reaction
(FeRR) nearly equally. Although planar electrode studies suggest an HER current density

                                           Page 50 of 127

higher than the FeOR current density can be achieved, this can only occur at large HER
overpotentials. More importantly, even in the most ideal scenario where all oxidative
current drives the FeOR and not the hydrogen oxidation reaction (HOR), and the fact that
the oxidation and reduction current of a photocatalyst particle must be equal in magnitude
with opposite sign, coated particles would operate at a current limited by mass transfer of
Fe(II) through the coating toward the particle surface. This can explain the reduced EQYs
for H2 evolution when coatings are present on the particles.
However, if this was the only effect of the coating, the decrease in EQY upon the addition
of Fe(III) would be similar for particles with and without coating, assuming (i) the addition
of Fe(III) does not perturb the transport mechanism of Fe(II) entering the coating and
reaching the particle surface, thus not altering mass-transfer-limited FeOR current and
(ii) particles without coating are already operating at a photocurrent limited by the FeOR.
The introduction of Fe(III) into the system provides an undesired pathway (FeRR) for
photoelectrons, reducing the maximum possible rate of the HER. The only reasoning for
a smaller decrease in EQY for the coated particles after the addition of Fe(III) is that the
coating has another effect of increasing the reaction selectivity of the HER over the FeRR,
indicating the concept of enhanced reaction selectivity through the addition of ultrathin
oxide coatings on catalyst reaction sites can be applied to not only planar electrodes but
also photocatalyst nanoparticles.
The observation that uncoated particles in the presence of Fe(II) result in baseline EQYs
for H2 evolution (black) that decrease significantly when Fe(III) is introduced (red) was
successfully replicated three times, as shown in Figure 43. And the observation that
addition of Fe(III) results in little change in EQYs for H2 evolution (green versus blue) from
Rh-doped SrTiO3 particles coated in TiOx was also successfully replicated three times.
The only reasoning for a replicable smaller decrease in quantum yield for the coated
particles after the addition of Fe(III), is that the coating has another effect of increasing
the reaction selectivity of the HER over Fe(III) reduction, indicating the concept of
enhanced reaction selectivity through the addition of ultrathin oxide coatings on catalyst
reaction sites can be applied to not only planar electrodes but also photocatalyst
nanoparticles. In total, there was an observation of a relative >2-fold quantum yield
savings due to the coatings.
The Irvine Team also explored the influence of Pt cocatalyst loading on H2 quantum yield
from illuminated Rh-doped SrTiO3 particles. HER photoactivity was found to be directly
related to the mass loading of Pt cocatalysts when methanol was used as a sacrificial
electron donor. When Fe(II) was used at a regenerative electron donor, the H2 quantum
yield was found to exhibit a local maximum at a Pt cocatalyst loading of 0.3 wt% Pt,
consistent with literature optimized Pt cocatalyst loading for Z-Scheme overall water
splitting systems. As a result, uncoated particles with cocatalyst loadings other than 0.3
wt% had low HER rates in the presence of Fe(II), and the HER rates of the corresponding
coated particles were further reduced to below the detection limit of our inline mass
spectrometry system, making it impossible to study the secondary effect of enhanced
reaction selectivty due to the TiOx coatings.
The Irvine Team and Hurst NLR EMN Node also attempted to evaluate the effect of SiOx
coating on 0.3 wt% Pt-cocatalyst-loaded Rh-doped SrTiO3 particles, but when Fe(II) was
used as the regenerative electron donor, the SiOx-coated particles exhibited HER rates

                                        Page 51 of 127

below our detection limit, despite having a slightly higher H2 quantum yield as compared
to TiOx-coated 0.3 wt% Pt-cocatalyst-loaded Rh-doped SrTiO3 particles when methanol
was used as a sacrificial electron donor. These results suggest that the conventional
wisdom that expected photoactivity for Z-Scheme overall water splitting can be quickly
screened using methanol as a sacrifical electron donor does not always hold. This could
be because reactions with redox mediators, such as Fe(II) oxidation on bare Rh-doped
SrTiO3, might be rate limiting compared with the HER on Pt, and that Fe(III) reduction is
in direct competition with the HER on the same Pt reaction sites.
The Irvine Team also explored the influence of Ru cocatalyst loading on Rh-doped SrTiO3
nanoparticles. The reduction in H2 evolution quantum yield upon the addition of Fe(III)
was more pronounced for these materials with TiOx coatings than for their Pt cocatalyst
counterpart, also with TiOx coatings (Figure 44). This observation provides an additional
explanation for the well-known
increase in the quantum yield for
H2 evolution when Ru cocatalysts
are used instead of Pt cocatalysts
on Rh-doped SrTiO3
nanoparticles. Different from the
conventional wisdom that Pt
exhibits superior activity over Ru
for water recombination (2 H2 + O2
→ 2 H2O), resulting in larger
quantum yields for H2 evolution
when Ru cocatalysts are used, an
alternative reasoning could be
that not only does Pt exhibit
superior activity to Ru for the
HER, but does so even more for
Fe(III) reduction. If that is the
case, then introduction of an
ultrathin oxide coating on Ru Figure 44. Ratio of H2 evolution quantum yield without and with

Task 3. Experimental Techniques
Significant Accomplishments and Conclusions
• We assessed >10 single-particles of Rh-doped SrTiO3 for their electrical properties via
  current–voltage and electron-beam-induced current measurements in the dry state, particles
  consisting of the same composition by scanning transmission electron microscopy and
  associated spectroscopies, and >5 sub-ten-particle-clusters for their photoelectrochemical
  behavior when wetted by electrolyte. Notably, we identified electron-beam-induced currents
  that are consistent with those expected under solar illumination conditions, where at least
  partial trap-state filling was observed to occurs and mobile charge-carrier transport changes
  from a trap-mediated process to quasi-ohmic conduction.
• We electrically characterized single-crystal BiVO4 nanoparticles and found a difference in
  Schottky barrier height between facets, although this difference in built-in electric potential is
  too small to significantly aid electron–hole pair separation when quasi-Fermi-level splitting is
  large enough to drive observed reactions.
• We quantitatively analyzed the correlation between photoluminescence and photocurrent for
  a single wetted BiVO4 particle and found a Spearman correlation coefficient of ρ = 0.70, which
  clearly indicates substantial correlation.
• We developed a high-throughput characterization workflow, validated its function using a
  model micron-scale Pt disc electrode and spots of Rh-doped SrTiO3 particles as thin films,
  and used it to scan an array of doped SrTiO3 materials with a throughput of 80 seconds per
  material, with capabilities to screen the photocatalytic activity of the large array of >1000
  materials spots generated within one day. While very little photocatalytic HER activity was
  observed from all materials spots analyzed using aqueous near-neutral pH conditions, arrays
  were also analyzed using fluorescence microscopy, which showed that materials with larger
  Sr:Ti ratios added during synthesis and/or higher dopant densities exhibited less
  photoluminescence at open circuit when wetted with a redox inactive aqueous electrolyte.
• Using mesoporous thin films of Rh-doped SrTiO3 particles, we performed
  photoelectrochemical measurements, which indicated that H2 oxidation at Pt cocatalysts was
  smaller when the particles were codoped with La.
• A time-resolved microwave conductivity spectroscopy system was developed to characterize
  mobile charge-carrier mobility and lifetime in dry photocatalyst particles cast as thin films,
  where we observed that signals for Rh-doped SrTiO3 particles were larger and longer-lived in
  the absence of catalysts, in comparison to those loaded with Pt or Ru cocatalysts.
• An unfunded collaborator (Dr. Junko Yano at LBNL) performed beamline X-ray spectroscopy
  measurements on Rh-doped SrTiO3 and Rh,La-codoped SrTiO3, which revealed that La is
  likely substituting for Sr, as expected, but that this results in significant deformation of the
  perovskite crystal lattice.

                                          Page 53 of 127

Subtask 3.1. Coupled Microscopy Techniques. Results from several microscopies
were used to determine structure–function relationships for individual doped SrTiO3
particles. This included the use of high-resolution scanning transmission electron
microscopy (STEM) with energy dispersive X-ray spectroscopy (EDS) and electron
energy loss spectroscopy (EELS) to quantify dopant distributions. To obtain necessary
atomic-scale resolution, small particles were synthesized using the polymerizable
complex or hydrothermal methods.
Quantify H2 evolution activity from single photocatalyst particles. Figure 45 shows a
comparison of the photocatalytic performance of Rh,La-codoped SrTiO3 particles (ID3A)
and Rh-doped SrTiO3 particles (ID6A). Literature reports suggest that codoping is
superior to doping with only Rh, because it maintains Rh in its photoactive 3+ oxidation
state. However, our data suggest otherwise. To gain a better understanding of this
outcome, the Irvine Team performed photoelectrochemistry (PEC) on mesoporous thin
films of the materials. Figure 46 shows that Rh,La-codoped SrTiO3 has inferior HER
activity compared to only Rh-doped SrTiO3, which is consistent with ensemble
photocatalytic data.

 (a) (b)

 Figure 45. Photocatalytic performance in 10% v/v methanol(aq) of 0.5 mg/mL doped SrTiO3
 particles with 0.3 wt% Pt photodeposited as HER cocatalysts: (a) EQY and (b) H2 evolution rate.

Without Pt cocatalysts, Rh-doped SrTiO3 photoelectrodes exhibited cathodic
photocurrents of ~1 µA/cm2 under continuous Ar purge, which increased by a factor of
~10 when an H2 purge was introduced, under both dark and light conditions. When Pt
was loaded as a cocatalyst, cyclic voltammograms under Ar remained nearly unchanged,
except for an earlier onset potential for the HER, but in the presence of H2 showed a
significant anodic current peak just positive of ~0 V vs RHE, presumably due to H2
oxidation at the surface of Rh-doped SrTiO3 or conceivably an artifact due to its presence
even on the cathodic sweep. Codoping with La resulted in an earlier onset potential for
the HER under Ar purge, with the same general shape voltammograms. Under H2 purge,
the anodic peak presumed to be due to H2 oxidation was far smaller, suggesting La
removes states in the photocatalyst particles that mediate charge transport and/or
catalyze H2 oxidation.
To further aid in revealing causes of observed ensemble performance, the Irvine Team
also developed a contactless method to characterize charge-carrier dynamics of
photocatalyst powders and mesoporous thin films using pump–probe time-resolved

After pulsed-laser excitation, the charge-carrier concentration increases, which alters the
imaginary part of the complex dielectric constant resulting in a decrease in reflected
microwave power due to absorption and heat dissipation. To explore the suitably of this
technique, the influence of cocatalyst loading was evaluated or dry Rh-doped SrTiO3
particles cast as mesoporous thin films (Figure 47b). Inclusion of Pt or Ru cocatalysts via
photochemical deposition resulted in a larger initial change in TRMC signal, suggestive
of a larger concentration and/or larger mobility for mobile charged species, possibly due
to localization of charges on the metallic cocatalysts.
To further investigate the unexpected observation that Rh,La-codoped SrTiO3 particles
are inferior to Rh-doped SrTiO3 particles, STEM was performed, along with EDS and
EELS, on Rh,La-codoped SrTiO3 particles (ID3) and La-doped SrTiO3 particles (ID45).
Unlike for Ir-doped SrTiO3 samples with heavy element Z-contrast, core-level ionization
of Rh was below the detection limit of the microscope. However, after careful background
subtraction, the obtained La dopant core-level ionization signal was weak, but did match

energy among the four samples, partially because it was synthesized by a different solid-
state method and resulted in larger particle sizes.

to focus on creating scattering models to fit the Rh K-edge data. To simulate Rh-doped
SrTiO3 (ID6) data, a standard perovskite SrTiO3 unit cell with Ti as the center atom was
first constructed and then the Ti atom was swapped with a Rh atom (Figure 49c). Data
fitting for ID6 was straightforward, and a reasonable fit was achieved. However, the data
fitting of ID3 required the introduction of an additional scattering atom, such as La, in
order to account for the additional peak in real space. In Figure 49d, a La atom
substituted one of the Sr atoms in the perovskite SrTiO3 unit cell structure, and by
adjusting the relative distance of this La atom and the center Ti atom, the Yano LBNL
Team was able to achieve a good fit. Nevertheless, the fitting result for Rh,La-codoped
SrTiO3 (ID3) can be further optimized, as the Yano LBNL Team observed that altering the
Sr-Ti atom distance can also create the additional scattering peak feature.
Library of structure–function relationships for composite photocatalyst particles. The Talin
SNL EMN Node pursued electron-beam-based characterization of carrier transport and
recombination in single photocatalyst nanoparticles. Following absorption of solar
radiation, photoexcited electron–hole pairs within individual nanoparticles separate and
transport to the surface to drive electrochemical reactions. Despite its importance to the
overall STH efficiency, the details of charge-carrier transport within photocatalysts
nanoparticles remain largely unknown. For bulk photoelectrodes, band bending at the
electrode|electrolyte interface or electrode|metallic cocatalyst|electrolyte interfaces is
frequently used to explain photoexcited charge-carrier separation and subsequent
transport of minority carriers to the surface. However, for sub-micron dimensions of
photocatalyst particles, it is no longer clear that such models remain applicable. Band
bending requires sufficient concentrations of mobile charge carriers, resulting in large
capacitances and thin space–charge regions, so that the electric potential can change
significantly within the particle volume. However, the nanoparticle may have insufficient
free carrier concentration due to surface or bulk traps, which is exacerbated by the high
surface-area-to-volume ratio of the nanoparticles.
To determine charge-carrier characteristics, the Talin SNL EMN Node developed a
method to contact individual particles using an electrical nanoprobe mounted inside of a
scanning electron microscope (SEM). In addition to collecting current versus voltage (I–
V) characteristics, this method enables the collection of electron-beam-induced current
(EBIC) signals. Like with photoexcitation, excitation by energetic electrons creates a large
density of electron–hole pairs.
Figure 50 shows nanoprobe electrical characterization of two different Rh-doped SrTiO3
photocatalyst nanoparticles, each with a different metal contact. The fact that the two I–
V characteristics are similar suggests that contact resistance is not likely dominating
observed behavior. This is further supported by the linear relationship for the data when
plotted as the square root of the current vs the applied potential (Figure 50d,h), and the
absence of a linear relationship on a semilogarithmic scale (Figure 50c,g), suggesting
that transport is not contact limited, for example, due to thermionic Schottky-junction
emission, but instead that the main transport mechanism is space–charge-limited
conduction (SCLC). This mechanism is expected to dominate when injection of carriers
at the contacts exceeds the ability of the material to transport the charge and is common
in semiconductors with low carrier concentration. This result implies that application of
simplified models developed for bulk photoelectrochemical semiconductor electrodes are

                                       Page 58 of 127

respectively. (e-h) Same results for particle 2, except that the probe is entirely W, without Au.

not necessarily appropriate for suspended photocatalyst nanoparticles. An important
consequence of transport in the SCLC regime is that it is determined entirely by the
dimensions, dielectric function, mobility, and applied potential bias, as follows,

Electronic transport characteristics were also examined for Rh-doped SrTiO3 particles
without versus with Pt cocatalysts (Figure 51). The presence of Pt cocatalysts did not
result in a significant difference in transport properties, except that in general smaller
particles were observed to be more conductive.
Injecting mobile charge carriers with the electron beam dramatically increases
conductivity of Rh-doped SrTiO3 nanoparticles, indicating they are “photoconductors”,
meaning that energetic electrons that generate electron–hole pairs by impact ionization,
similar to that observed when light is absorbed, results in a decrease in resistance (Figure
52a,b). Without electron-beam irradiation, the I–V profile of the nanoparticle (Figure 52c)
has a slope of 𝛽 = 2.5 on a log–log plot. When the electron beam is rastered across the

and 𝛽 is lowered to 1.02 ± 0.02. A 𝛽 of 1 indicates that transport is Ohmic. Note that the
increase in conductivity is proportional to the beam-injected mobile charge-carrier density,
which is controlled by the beam current setting. A linear I–V profile, along with a linear
relation between conductivity and injected mobile charge-carrier density, is consistent
with “photoconduction.” Also note that, although conductivity measurements driven by

The mobile charge-carrier lifetime can be determined from the EBIC resistance (inverse
of the slope of the green fit, Figure 52c) of the particle, 𝑅𝛽 = 118 ± 1 GΩ,

where 𝐼𝑏 is the beam current of 63 pA, reduced by the ratio of the projected particle area

A mobile charge-carrier lifetime of 1.2 ps is extracted from these material and
experimental parameters. This low value is consistent with the low STH efficiency of these
photocatalytic nanoparticles. This short recombination time corresponds to a
recombination length, 𝐿 = √𝜇𝑘𝑇𝜏/𝑒 of 5.5 nm. Thus, within a typical 170 nm-radius
particle (mean radius of particles), only the outer 10% of its volume will generate
photocarriers which can contribute meaningfully to the HER. Light absorbed within the
inner ~90% of the particle will generate electron–hole pairs that recombine before charge
separating, limiting the maximum internal quantum yield to ~10%. In this diffusion-limited
regime, smaller particles will have higher STH efficiency than larger particles since the
active volume of a smaller particle will be a larger fraction of its total volume.
The Talin SNL EMN Node used the EBIC method to further substantiate our hypothesis
that there are no extended space–charge regions within the photocatalyst nanoparticles
to separate generated electron–hole pairs. EBIC can be applied as a scanning electron
probe technique to directly map electron–hole charge separation efficiency within a
semiconductor, and has been used extensively to map space–charge regions within
nanostructures with sub-10 nm spatial resolution. EBIC images of Rh-doped SrTiO3
nanoparticles at zero applied potential bias (Figure 52d) reveal a relatively weak signal
that is uniform across the particle, indicating that built-in electric fields are too weak to
separate electron–hole pairs. All EBIC contrast within the 0 V image is below 300 pA, less
than a factor of two larger than the beam current, consistent with secondary electron
emission dominating current. The maximum EBIC signal within this image occurs over
the metallic gold probe, which cannot separate electron–hole pairs, providing further
evidence that secondary electron generation is responsible for contrast in this image.
When +5 V is applied, strong EBIC is measured near the gold probe tip (Figure 52e,f)
which switches sign alongside bias direction. This relatively strong EBIC, which is an
order-of-magnitude larger than the beam current, indicates that application of an applied
potential bias results in efficient separation of beam-generated electron–hole pairs.
The space–charge region of a semiconductor nanoparticle contacting metal, or
surrounded by electrolyte, is geometrically limited when the radius of the particle is less
than the Debye screening length, 𝐿𝐷 , within the semiconductor. The maximum potential

                                       Page 61 of 127

 𝑘𝑇 𝑑 2
drop is limited to 𝑉𝑆 = ( ) . The small built-in electric potential observed for Rh-doped
 24 𝐿𝐷
SrTiO3 nanoparticles is likely due to their low mobile charge-carrier concentration within
the photocatalyst nanoparticles. Transport in these particles is trap-mediated and thus
the mobile charge-carrier concentration is very low, and the Debye screening length
correspondingly very long compared to the particle diameter. In this limit, the electric
potential difference across the particle will be negligible. This is in contrary to what is
typically reported in the literature for possible means by which photocatalysis particles
operate. Our results suggest that a substantial reduction in trap density is needed to
improve the STH efficiency for these Rh-doped SrTiO3 photocatalyst particles.

 Energy band diagram for Rh-doped SrTiO3 with oxygen vacancy and Rh trap levels indicated. (c)

Temperature-dependent transport measurements were also performed on single SrTiO3
nanoparticles and were fitting to a trap-assisted SCLC model (Figure 53a). A trap-state
energy of 𝐸𝑇 = 0.34 ± 0.02 eV below the conduction band was extracted. Given the 3.2
eV bandgap of SrTiO3, this state corresponds to an optical transition of ~2.8 eV, which is
close to the value calculated for trapped polarons from oxygen vacancies (Figure 53b)
and the approximate photon energy observed by photoluminescence spectroscopy for
each of undoped and Rh-doped SrTiO3 nanoparticles (Figure 53c), confirming this state
is not formed due to Rh. Information about the bulk carrier lifetime can be extracted from
the photoluminescence data and is consistent with photocurrent measurements made on
individual nanoparticles. The intensity (𝐼) of a photoluminescence peak is proportional to
𝐼~ , where 𝜏𝑟𝑎𝑑 and 𝜏𝑛𝑜𝑛𝑟𝑎𝑑 are the radiative and non-radiative lifetimes,
respectively. In the limit that 𝜏𝑛𝑜𝑛𝑟𝑎𝑑 ≪ 𝜏𝑟𝑎𝑑 , the intensity of a peak is proportional to
 . Rhodium doping decreases the intensity of the 𝑉𝑂2+ polaron peak by a factor of
~100. Assuming that 𝜏𝑟𝑎𝑑 is unaffected by Rh mid-gap states, 𝜏𝑛𝑜𝑛𝑟𝑎𝑑 will be reduced by
this same factor, which agrees well with our previous single-particle “photoconduction”
measurements.
Electronic transport characteristics were also examined for a Rh-doped SrTiO3 particle
under high vacuum versus with a pressure of water vapor of 0.5 mbar (Figure 54). No

                                            Page 62 of 127

  Figure 54. A single Rh-doped SrTiO3 nanoparticle probed in the SEM containing an atmosphere of 0.5
  mbar water vapor. Insignificant changes in both (a) dark current and (b) photocurrent behavior support
  that electronic species transport is bulk limited, and that the photocarrier lifetime is unchanged.

significant difference was observed without or with electron-beam irradiation. These
observations are consistent with our previous conclusion that charge transport in Rh-
doped SrTiO3 nanoparticles is determined by bulk traps, and not surface states, despite
the large surface-area-to-volume ratio of the nanoparticles.
The short carrier lifetime we observe for Rh-doped SrTiO3 requires a quantized approach
to charge transport, since at any given time there will be at most just a single photo-
excited electron–hole pair within a nanoparticle. Even for the unrealistic scenario where
every photon within the solar spectrum (1000 W/m2) is absorbed by a 170-nm-radius
particle, an electron–hole pair would only be generated every ~2.5 ns, on average. With
a ~1 ps recombination time, the probability of two electron–hole pairs interacting within a
nanoparticle is approximately 1/2,500. Modeling charge transport with a standard drift–
diffusion model assumes that a large density of photocarriers thermally equilibrate with
the lattice, but this assumption is not justified unless the carrier lifetime is well in excess
of 2.5 ns. This new information should be considered when developing accurate models
for photocatalytic performance.

Figure 56. Nano-electrical probing of different facets of a BiVO4 nanoparticle: (a) transport characteristics,
(b,d) scanning electron microscopy images, and (c,e) corresponding electron-beam-induced current data.
A difference in transport characteristics implies a small difference in work function between facets, however
this potential is not large enough to separate electron–hole pairs, which does occur as evidenced by
measurement of electron-beam-induced currents.

The Talin SNL EMN Node also characterized electrical properties of single BiVO4
nanoparticles. Transport characteristics (Figure 56a) between facets are overlapping in
reverse bias, but diverge in forward bias, implying a difference in Schottky barrier height,
or work function, between facets. However, electron-beam-induced current maps taken
simultaneously show no significant built-in electric field between the gold probe tip and
the nanoparticle for either facet, implying that the difference in built-in potential is too small
to significantly aid charge separation of electron–hole pairs.

Subtask 3.2. High-Throughput Techniques. Arrays of doped ABO3 particles were
synthesized via a high-throughput deposition protocol. However, rapid screening of such
an array for photocatalytic activity has not been previously reported. Therefore, it is critical
to leverage our team’s expertise to develop methods of rapidly analyzing large arrays of
new materials for photocatalytic activity.
Quantify H2 evolution activity for an array of ABO3 particles. Arrays of ABO3 particles with
>1000 different dopant compositions were deposited onto insulating glass, quartz, and
silicon substrates, and thus we developed a workflow to measure their photocatalytic
activity. The use of local electrochemical measurements, such as SECM, can measure

                                              Page 64 of 127

the H2 produced from
individual samples on
these arrays by using
a Pt

demonstrate the

samples deposited

(known) active H2
evolving particles of varying photocatalyst loading. Rh-doped SrTiO3 particles containing
Pt cocatalysts were drop-cast from an isopropanol solution into 6 spot sizes with varying
amounts of material, as seen in Figure 57a. These particles were deposited onto two
different substrates: indium-doped tin oxide (ITO)/glass and ITO-free glass. The
conductive ITO substrate, combined with the use of a counter electrode to carry out
oxidation, allows for control of the potential of the photocatalyst-containing substrate and
direct measurement of electrochemical signals from the samples, as illustrated
schematically in Figure 57b. The insulating glass substrate more closely approximates
the electronic conditions of photocatalyst particles in solution, which are not subjected to
an external applied bias or a large density of metallic states. However, this realism comes
at the expense of not being able to directly measure the electrochemical signal from the
photocatalyst-containing substrate. Thus, photocatalyst activity can only be measured by
detecting H2 using the UME sensor, as shown in Figure 57c. Furthermore, the screening
measurements carried out on the insulating substrate require the use of an electron donor
species (methanol was used for this study) such that both oxidation and reduction are
carried out on the photocatalyst sample.
To detect H2 evolved from the substrate, a 10 μm diameter platinum UME probe was
placed 25 μm above an H2 evolving sample “spot” of interest, and its potential was held
0.5 V vs RHE. At this potential, the UME is able to oxidize the evolved H2 while the
background signal associated with conversion of other redox active species is kept at a
minimum. After the background signal from the UME stabilized in the dark, a UV pen light
source (λ = 365 nm, FWHM ~15 nm, 6 W electrical power, focused spot size ~1 mm2)
was used to illuminate the photocatalyst particles for 5 s intervals. A typical chopped light

A schematic of the control scheme can be seen in Figure 61a. Initially the tip will be
positioned over the first sample, and lowered to the correct z height. Chronoamperometry
chopped light measurements are then completed, before retracting the tip and moving to
the next sample. Each (x, y) movement is completed with high-speed control to minimize
downtime between scans. The chronoamperometry scan can easily be adjusted to reduce
scan time, for higher throughput, or lengthen scan time, for better signal averaging.
Chronoamperometry data for each scan was written to separate CSV files, with location
metadata stored. This setup allows for automation of data analysis in addition to data
collection.
The retrofitted apparatus and control scheme were tested using a grid of 700 µm Pt disks
on p-type Si (1 – 10 Ω-cm). This sample allows for testing of both the individual chopped
light parameters as well as the positional control needed for high-throughput screening.
A high-throughput scan was completed in aqueous 25 mM FeSO4 + 25 mM Fe2(SO4)3 +
100 mM Na2SO4 + 50 mM H2SO4 at pH 1.5 over a 4 x 5 grid of Pt|Si disks with a 5th
column as a bare control (Figure 61b). The tip was held at 0.1 V vs RHE, and the
substrate was held at -0.2 V vs RHE. The tip and substrate potential were chosen from
cyclic voltammetry experiments to both maximize substrate photoresponse as well as tip
collection signal. Data from a single chopped light measurement is shown in Figure 61c,
which demonstrates a positive (oxidative) response associated with the oxidation of H2 at
the tip. Differential tip current was calculated from the difference between tip current
measured under illumination and in the dark, using the average current recorded over the
last 0.5 s of each interval. The resulting differential tip current is plotted in Figure 61d for
(x, y) locations corresponding to each of the 20 samples, as well as 5 “blank” locations
for which no disk was deposited. The majority of the samples exhibited a consistent ~ 10
nA increase in the tip current when the light was turned on, except for the blank samples
in the last column of Figure 61b, where a tip current of ~0 nA was recorded over the bare

                                         Page 67 of 127

Si. This result confirms that the differential tip signal measured for the other samples is
associated with H2 evolving from the Pt disks. However, there are large discrepancies
associated with the recorded differential tip signal for the 1st row and 5th row of Pt disks
in Figure 61d. The substrate photoresponse (Figure 61e) exhibits a drastic reduction
over the 5th row, as well as large variation in the 1st column, and thus we attribute the
aforementioned discrepancies in Figure 61d to non-uniformities in the substrate
geometry and/or changes to local illumination intensity that are affected by shadowing
effects from the Teflon sample holder and/or the scanning microelectrode assembly as it

                                                Page 68 of 127

sweeps across the sample. Fortunately, these complications are not necessarily an issue
for the high-throughput photocatalytic particle measurements, which only require local
illumination of a single sample at a time. Another consideration is the substrate-probe
distance (d) changing over the large grid array. It is important to minimize d, which is
normally 2–3 times larger than the tip diameter, to increase product collection efficiency.
Any small variations in substate horizontal slope can alter the gap significantly, as total
grid size is on the order of mm. This issue will be addressed in the next report through
use of a constant separation distance “soft-tip” probe, which contacts the surface.
Nonetheless, these scans demonstrate the ability to automate the measurement of 25
samples with an average of 157 sec per sample, and data analysis. This corresponds to
~180 samples for a full workday of 8 h. A large portion of the scan time was in the
chronoamperometry measurement, which will be optimized further for photocatalyst
samples, where signals will be smaller and require smaller equilibration times.
This methodology was then extended to doped SrTiO3 particle grid samples. Minor
modifications were implemented to the setup including the use of a soft-tip probe and
relocation of the apparatus onto an air table. Both modifications were done to reduce
extraneous signal associated with tip–substrate separation distance and mechanical
vibrations. Validation of these modifications was completed on previously fabricated grid
samples of both Pt disks and drop-casted Rh-doped SrTiO3 particles.
High-throughput scans were then completed on SrTiO3 particle grid arrays immersed in
aqueous 100 mM Na2SO4 + 10 % v/v methanol at pH 7. At each particle location, a 40 s
chopped light chronoamperometry experiment was completed with a tip potential of 0.8
V vs RHE and 20 s of equilibration time before the light was chopped on/off for 2 s
intervals. A UV-Pen light source (λ = 365 nm, FWHM: ~15 nm, electrical power: 6 W,
focused spot size: ~1 mm2) was used for illumination. A representative scan is shown in
Figure 62a, where the HER activity is correlated to the difference in tip current between
light on and off events. However, as can be seen in Figure 62b, erroneous current events
occur during some of the 24 scans over a 6 x 4 grid of samples, causing it to be nearly
impossible to accurately estimate activity. A rudimentary filtering process was used to
correct for this error. Moreover, rather than averaging all five light on/off occurrences, only
points where dark current was similar to the previous value were averaged.
Figure 62c shows the compilation of the filtered differential (light minus dark) tip current
over 4 grids of 6 x 4 samples, with color gradient used to visual higher activity. Boxed
values correspond to samples that were overlapped during the smaller grid samples.
Given that the transient current change is 1 – 5 pA over these 2 second time periods,
little-to-no photoresponse was seen for any of the 77 samples evaluated. Experiments
were repeated on this sample, as well as another grid sample that also demonstrated
minimal activity (data not shown for brevity and to avoid redundancy).
It is not surprising that little-to-no activity was observed for these samples because (i)
cocatalysts were not present, which are known to improve the kinetics of the HER, and
(ii) at near-neutral pH conditions there is an increased overpotential required for the HER
through water reduction. Nevertheless, these scans demonstrate the ability to automate
the measurement and analysis of 96 samples (77 were unique), at an average of 80
seconds per sample spot.

The Talin SNL EMN Node measured photoelectrochemical (PEC) photocurrent and
photoluminescence (PL) intensity maps simultaneously on BiVO4 nanoparticles
dispersed on a glassy carbon substrate in an aqueous 0.5 M KH2PO4 buffer at pH 7 with
the electrode potential held at +0.7 V vs an Ag wire pseudo-reference electrode and 532
nm continuous-wave laser excitation. As seen in Figure 63, there is a correlation between
PL and PEC characteristics. The data in Figure 63d were used to calculate the Spearman
correlation coefficient (ρ), which measures the strength and direction of the correlation
between ranked pairs. We found that ρ = 0.70, which suggests substantial correlation
between PL intensity and PEC.

After characterizing the particular array shown in Figure 63a, three additional arrays were
tested (not shown). These additional arrays were each synthesized at a different
temperature (450, 650, and 1075 °C) and focused on Ir and Rh dopants only. SPCM
measurements were attempted on select samples from the array in Figure 63a, but we
encountered challenges related to the spatial resolution of our optical microscope.
All four arrays were then shared with the Columbia Team for further PEC assessment via
SECM. Minor modifications to the SECM setup were completed to reduce background
noise through the use of a USB isolator. Experiments were additional completed with
acidic methanol solution, and with the presence of photodeposited Pt cocatalysts with the
aim to increase photoresponse signals. High-throughput scans were completed in
aqueous 100 mM Na2SO4 + 50mM H2SO4 + 10 % v/v methanol at pH 1.5 on four different
grid samples, with varying annealing conditions. At each particle location, a 40 s chopped
light chronoamperometry experiment was completed with a tip potential of 0.8 V vs RHE
and 20 s of equilibration time before the light was chopped on/off for 2 s intervals. A UV-
Pen light source (λ = 365 nm, FWHM ~15 nm, 6 W electrical power, focused spot size ≈
1 mm2) was used for illumination. Samples were measured in smaller grids (typically 6 x
6) to avoid aging effects that are more likely to arise for longer measurement time periods.

                                      Page 73 of 127

Task 4. Numerical Models
Significant Accomplishments and Conclusions
• We developed and validated a single-particle-scale model for individual photocatalyst
  particles that considers charge-carrier generation, recombination, and transport processes
  within the semiconductor material, semiconductor optical properties, and interfacial reaction
  kinetics at the buried semiconductor–catalyst interface. Results revealed that while the total
  current produced increases as the particle size increases, due to an increased absorption of
  incident photons, the collection efficiency that normalizes the current with the total rate of
  photon absorption within the particle decreases, resulting in decreased predicted internal
  quantum yields.
• We modified the equivalent circuit modeling framework that is commonly used in the
  photoelectrochemistry community and includes ideal (photo)diode semiconductor behavior
  and electrocatalysis that follow Bulter–Volmer kinetics to quantitatively account for the effects
  of two redox reactions undergoing desired and undesired reactions with mobile electronic
  carriers in the semiconductor, experimental values for partition coefficients and mass-transfer
  limited current densities as a function of redox shuttle concentration that we measured
  electrochemically for ultrathin coatings, and the effects of having an ensemble of
  independently operating photocatalyst particles that absorb light based on the Beer–Bouger–
  Lambert law. We performed parametric sweeps that included the subset of properties of
  various coating parameters – including thickness, species permeability (as solubility partition
  coefficient and effective diffusivity), surface kinetic parameters – and boundary layer thickness
  to determine those that resulted in 3%, 5% and 10% STH efficiencies for an ensemble of
  optically thin light absorber slabs.
• We designed and built a simplified reactor prototype, with polycarbonate walls and an acrylic
  base that was painted black on the side facing the illumination, which included an array of
  thermocouples spanning its height of 10 cm to measure the one-dimensional temperature
  profile, to assess the extent that solar-thermally-driven convection can provide an additional
  means of mass transfer for redox shuttle species. Experiments were performed either by
  heating from the bottom of the reactor using a hot plate set to ~35°C or by illuminating from
  the top with light from a solar simulator at an irradiance of 0.3, 0.5, and 1 Sun (1 kW/m2).
  Results indicate that the temperature gradient is significantly larger near the bottom surface
  than in the bulk solution and directly correlates with light intensity, which was corroborated by
  injecting a colored dye into the solution near the bottom of the prototype and acquiring images
  at different times afterward.
• We developed a reactor-scale modeling framework with coupled flow, heat transfer, and mass
  transfer to assess the influence that natural convection has on transport of redox mediator
  species within a single reaction compartment and between two Z-Scheme reaction
  compartments in the presence of a membrane separator. The model was validated to a
  satisfactory extent against experimental measurements, and was then used to determine the
  minimum concentrations of redox shuttles needed to operate at specific STH efficiencies (i.e.
  1%, 3%, 5%, 10%), where results point to clear benefits in terms of enhanced mass transfer
  in the presence of natural convection, even despite the presence of a separator between
  reactor compartments.

                                         Page 74 of 127

Subtask 4.1. Particle-Scale Model. A continuum-level model was developed to
determine the sensitivity of modeling parameters and particle size on the efficiency of
charge collection. It includes a two-dimensional circular semiconductor particle coated
with a dense cocatalyst that forms a buried junction on the surface of the semiconductor
in an aqueous solution, and continuity equations for charge and electron/hole
(a) (b)

Figure 66. Modeling domain, governing conservation equations and boundary conditions for the particle-
scale model to compute collection efficiencies and to determine spatial profiles.

concentrations (Figure 66a). Notably, the cocatalyst was assumed to cover ~30% of the
surface area of the photocatalyst particle and was assumed thin enough relative to the
particle diameter that it was treated like a surface, and the electrolyte surrounding the
particle was not explicitly modeled. Key input material parameters and their baseline
values, chosen based on properties reported for crystalline silicon, are tabulated in Figure
66b. The semiconductor–electrolyte and the semiconductor–cocatalyst interfaces were
modeled as Schottky junctions with a fixed barrier height and simplified Butler–Volmer
electrochemical kinetics was applied to capture the rate of electron transfer between the
cocatalyst and the electrolyte. Only the OER was modeled with the kinetic parameters
shown in Figure 66b. The volumetric generation rate for the charge-carriers, 𝐺𝑛𝑝 , was
computed by assuming a Beer–Bouger–Lambert law absorption profile within the particle
and by assuming that all above-bandgap photons are perfectly absorbed by the
semiconductor. The charge collection efficiency, 𝜂collection , which is related to IQY, was
defined as the ratio of the rate at which electrons/holes leave the surface of the particle
to the rate at which photons are absorbed within the volume of the semiconductor as
follows,

 ∭ 𝐺𝑛𝑝 𝑑𝑉

This model was validated by establishing favorable comparisons, to within 0.1% error,
between the results predicted for a planar electrode with a dense electrocatalyst.6
Simulations assuming perfect redox reaction selectivity. First, we focused on numerical
models that implemented only the desired reactions at the catalyst–electrolyte interface.

                                           Page 75 of 127

Figure 67a,b shows the spatial profiles of the electric potential and the current density
for a particle of diameter 200 nm with baseline input parameters from Figure 66b. The
largest drop in the electric potential occurs between the surface and the bulk of the
photocatalytic particle, indicating that effects due to band-bending are relevant for this
sized particle. The current density and electric fields are concentrated around the
cocatalyst as it occupies a much smaller surface area as compared to the bare surface
of the semiconductor in contact with the electrolyte. The collection efficiency for the
baseline case was determined to be 54% and was found to be acutely sensitive to the
interfacial exchange current density modeled for the electrocatalytic reactions; the
exchange current density modeled for the cocatalyst depends on the type of the surface,
temperature, and surface concentrations of the redox species. The surface recombination
velocities modeled also impacted the predicted efficiencies, but to a much lesser extent
as compared to the interfacial catalytic parameters.
Because particle agglomerates have been reported in the size ranges of 100 nm – 1000
nm for various Z-Scheme particle suspension systems,7 the model was also used to
determine the influences of particle size on the collection efficiencies and the total
electron/hole current produced at the surface of an individual particle (Figure 68). These
results indicate that the absorption efficiency is inversely related to the particle size. This
efficiency decreases as the diameter increases because the surface area per unit volume
of the particle decreases. However, due to the increased external surface area of the
particle to absorb incident photons, the total electron/hole current at the particle surface

Parametric studies were performed to assess the
influences of: (a) semiconductor absorption
coefficient, (b) potential barrier implemented at
the semiconductor|electrolyte interface, and (c)
incident photon flux. Only a single spherical
particle was modeled, without spectrally Figure 68. Effect of particle size on the
 charge-collection efficiency and the total
resolving the absorption coefficient of the electron/hole current generated at the
semiconductor. The semiconductor modeled was particle surface.
assumed to have a bandgap energy of 2 eV, all
above bandgap photons were assumed to be absorbed based on a spatially-varying
exponential Beer–Bouger–Lambert law profile within the volume of the semiconductor
particle, and surface reflectance losses were not considered (Figure 66). Models were
used to quantify: (1) a collection efficiency or the internal quantum yield/efficiency per
particle (IQEparticle), which compares the rate of charge carriers leaving the surface to the
rate at which they are absorbed within the volume of the particle, and (2) an external
quantum yield/efficiency per particle (EQEparticle), that quantifies the rate of the charge
carriers leaving the surface to the incident rate of photons on the particle surface. While
the IQEparticle is indicative of how efficient the transport and interfacial kinetics are in
collecting and separating mobile charge-carrier species from the bulk to the particle
surface, the EQEparticle, in addition to transport and kinetics, also accounts for the material
bandgap above which absorption of the incident photons occurs, and the efficiency of
volumetric absorption within the material. With increase in the volumetric absorption
coefficient of the bulk semiconductor material, the EQEparticle increases due to the
increased absorption of the above band-gap photons within the volume of the particles
and the associated increase in the rate of charge-carrier generation (Figure 69a).
However, the IQEparticle decreases with increase in the absorption coefficient beyond 106
m-1, because the current generated at the surface does not increase at the same rate as
the rate of charge-carrier absorption within the semiconductor volume. This outcome is
an artifact of the absorption length scales (<0.1 µm) being much lesser than the particle
size, which results in increased bulk recombination of the photogenerated mobile charge-
carrier species. Figure 69b,d depicts the influence of varying the potential barrier
between the semiconductor and the electrolyte. By lowering the potential barrier for
electron transfer, the mobile charge-carrier species produced within the volume are more
easily separated due to the enhanced electric field to drive the holes and electrons to the
respective surfaces. When the potential barrier becomes large, the radial electric field
results in increased bulk recombination of the mobile charge-carrier species produced.
Finally, we also varied the intensity of photons incident on the surface of the particle
(Figure 68c,e), where an intensity fraction of 1 corresponds to 1 Sun irradiance. EQEparticle
remains somewhat constant with respect to the intensity fraction, as the total current
generated per particle (Figure 69e) increases nearly linearly with increase in the incident

Simulations of competing redox reactions and selective coatings. With a suitable model
for a single particle that captures its bulk and interfacial properties, we sought to introduce
interfacial redox reactions, including redox selectivities, first via a model based on a load-
line analysis. Traditional zero-dimensional equivalent circuit models for
photoelectrochemical devices model an ideal diode with the desired pair of
electrochemical reactions. We modified this framework (Figure 71) to account for
competing reactions at each band (dotted-line resistors in Figure 71b) in addition to the
desired reactions (solid-line resistors in Figure 71b). Figure 71a considers the H2-
evolving photocatalyst with the Fe(III)/Fe(II) redox mediator and parallel current pathways

in the respective parallel

comparable (STH = 20.8%) when the surface was modeled to be selective toward
oxidation of the redox shuttle with a,RS = 0.9. For j0,H2 < 1 mA cm-2 (10 A m-2) the
maximum STH efficiency decreases as c,H2 decreases; when j0,H2 = 0.001 mA cm-2 (0.01
A m-2), the maximum STH efficiency for c,H2 = 0.9 is 19.1%, for c,H2 = 0.7 it is 14.5%,
and for c,H2 = 0.5 is 4.5% (not shown on Figure 72). When the a,RS value decreases to
0.7, meaning that the anodic reaction for the redox shuttle is less favorable, the optimal
STH efficiency values dramatically reduce to 5.8% for c,H2 = 0.9 and 4.6% for c,H2 = 0.7
(not shown on Figure 72), even for the largest exchange current densities modeled for
the H2 reactions. If a,RS further decreases to 0.5, predicted STH efficiencies for c,H2 =
0.9 and c,H2 = 0.7 are negative unless the exchange current density for the RS reaction
is larger than 1 mA cm-2 (10 A m-2). This indicates the need to limit the extent at which
the H2 oxidation reaction can compete with the shuttle oxidation reaction at the valence
band. As the exchange current density of the shuttle reaction is varied (Figure 72), the
tradeoff between rapid kinetics and mass-transport limitations is clear by maximum STH
efficiencies being reached for the largest j0,H2 value and j0,RS = 0.1 mA cm-2 (1 A m-2).
Overall, these results are consistent with our previous publication3 and indicate the
importance of asymmetric charge-transfer coefficients to favor the desired redox
reactions in order to obtain large STH efficiencies, and highlight that there is an optimal
redox shuttle exchange current density that balances the benefits of improved redox
shuttle electrocatalysis with increased driving forces for competing redox shuttle reactions
for large values of its exchange current density.
To further elucidate the driving forces for the competing reactions, Figure 73 shows the
current–voltage curves for the case that considered a,RS = 0.9, c,H2 = 0.9, j0,H2 = 1 mA
cm-2 (10 A m-2), and j0,RS = 0.1 mA cm-2 (1 A m-2). The operating current density and
potential are represented by the solid black marker on the diode curve, which was
obtained by numerically solving the coupled non-linear equations that capture the diode
properties, Butler–Volmer electrochemical kinetics, and mass-transfer limited current
densities. Based on the currents and the overpotentials computed for each redox species
in the valence and conduction bands, we plot the corresponding points on the polarization
curves for the redox reactions. At the conduction band, selective H2 evolution takes place

Figure 74 demonstrates the influences of the H2
and RS exchange current densities on the selectivity of the reactions at the conduction
and valence bands for c,H2 = 0.7 and a,RS = 0.9. In Figure 74a, the overall H2 selectivity
decreases with increasing j0,RS because of a decrease in the kinetic overpotential
associated with the shuttle reaction, such that the competing shuttle reduction can
contribute up to 50% of the conduction band current density for the largest value of j0,H2
modeled. For (a) (b)
smaller values of
j0,H2, the decrease
in selectivity is due
in part to a
decrease in the
kinetic
overpotential of the
shuttle reaction but
also due to an
increase in the
kinetic Figure 74. Selectivity plots for c,H2 = 0.7 and a,RS = 0.9 for (a) the HER and (b)
overpotential redox shuttle oxidation (1 A m-2 = 0.1 mA cm-2).

                                             Page 81 of 127

associated with the HER. Figure 74a can also be used to explain the trends in STH
efficiency shown in Figure 72. For large j0,RS values, the decrease in STH efficiency can
be correlated to a decrease in H2 selectivity. However, for smaller j0,RS values, even
though the H2 selectivity approaches 100%, the selectivity for oxidizing the redox shuttle
diminishes (Figure 74b) and the overall STH efficiency decreases over this range of
values for j0,RS. Two factors contribute to this outcome: as j0,RS decreases, the kinetic
overpotentials for the redox shuttle oxidation reaction increase at the valence band,
resulting in (i) shifting the operating potential for the diode closer to its open-circuit
potential, resulting in lower operating current density and therefore lower overall STH
efficiency, and (ii) the larger kinetic overpotentials for the redox shuttle also lead to a
larger driving force for the H2 oxidation reaction, thus decreasing the net rate of H2
evolution. Overall, desired selectivity for H2 evolution increases when exchange current
densities for the shuttle reaction j0,RS < 0.1 mA cm-2 (1 A m-2) and for the H2 reaction j0,H2
is large, and desired selectivity for redox shuttle oxidation increases when exchange
current densities for the shuttle reaction j0,RS > 0.01 mA cm-2 (0.1 A m-2) and for the H2
reaction j0,H2 is larger.
Given our single-particle model that can capture semiconductor-based properties and our
equivalent circuit model that can couple semiconductor behavior to interfacial
electrochemical redox reactions and transport effects, the Michigan Team expanded the
equivalent circuit model to include reaction selectivities based on experimental values
measured for both the ultrathin SiOx coatings developed by the Columbia Team, and
more generally Z-Scheme photocatalytic systems with competing redox reactions. The
current–overpotential behavior with these coatings applies the measured values for the
partition coefficients, 𝑆𝑅 and 𝑆𝑂 , in Equation 20, and the mass-transfer-limited current
densities in Equation 21.

The partition coefficient compares the ratio of the species concentrations at the interface
between the electrode and the bulk electrolyte, and at the electrode surface, with and
without a selective coating on the electrode. These equations capture how the selective
coating affects the transport of species from the bulk to the electrode surface and the
reactivity of the species at the electrode surface. All required parameters in Equations
20 and 21 were obtained from measured cyclic voltammograms obtained for various
coatings developed by the Columbia Team (Table 4). We modeled a worst-case scenario
for the H2 oxidation currents by assuming that the coating did not alter the limiting currents
for H2 oxidation.

Figure 75a shows the calculated STH efficiencies for a single light absorber with a
bandgap of 1.55 eV and with various coatings that were developed and tested by the
Columbia Team. In this analysis, the exchange current density for the H2 reaction varied
as 10-5 – 100 A m-2, and a charge-transfer coefficient, c,H2 = 0.9, was assumed.
For these parameters, the redox shuttle reactions were mass-transfer limited for reactions
at both the valence band and the conduction band. Only the coating of 5 nm with a 120
min cure time shows a predicted positive value for STH efficiency, reaching 0.08%,
whereas the other coatings all have predicted negative values for STH efficiency due to
more H2 being oxidized than produced. The cathodic limiting current density for redox
shuttle reduction at the conduction band was the smallest for the 5 nm, 120 min cure time
coating. Overall, the cathodic limiting current densities for the Fe(III)/Fe(II) redox shuttle
has a direct impact on STH efficiency and H2 selectivity at the conduction band (Figure
75b). Both STH efficiency and H2 selectivity decrease with increase in the magnitude of

Figure 77 shows the model predicted STH efficiencies as function of the ratio of the
thickness of the coating and the boundary layer, and the kinetic parameters. For a fixed
set of limiting currents of the various redox reactions, shuttle redox kinetics with a larger
asymmetry in the charge-transfer coefficients results in larger and more positive STH
efficiencies. As the ratio of the thickness of the coating to the boundary layer increases
(from the dark to the lighter shades for a fixed color), STH efficiency decreases, because
the limiting currents for all the redox species (Fe(III), Fe(II), H+, and H2) decreases. There
is a drop-off in the STH efficiency values because of the inversely proportional

coating thickness, 𝑡coat , and species permeabilities (𝑃𝑒,𝑖 ), all of which greatly affect limiting
current densities for all species (Equation 23). A relative comparison of the mass-transfer
coefficients in the boundary layer, 𝑘𝑚,𝐵𝐿 , and in the coating, 𝑘𝑚,𝑐𝑜𝑎𝑡 , (Equations 24 and
25) help identify whether the bulk flow or transport in the coating limits mass-transfer
fluxes. When 𝑘𝑚,𝐵𝐿 ≫ 𝑘𝑚,𝑐𝑜𝑎𝑡 , transport through the coating limits mass transport,
whereas when 𝑘𝑚,𝐵𝐿 ≪ 𝑘𝑚,𝑐𝑜𝑎𝑡 , diffusion in the boundary layer limits mass transport and

As shown in the load-line analysis in Figure 78b, as 𝑃𝑒,𝑖 increases from 10-13 to 10-11 m2
s-1, the H2 behavior shifts from requiring an overpotential of approximately 0.1 V to
reaching large current densities at potentials larger than 0 V. For the redox shuttle
reactions, as 𝑃𝑒,𝑖 increases from 10-13 to 10-11 m2 s-1, overpotentials for redox shuttle
reduction at the conduction band decrease whereas overpotentials for redox shuttle
oxidation at the valence band increase, due to the increase in mass-transfer overpotential
required. Overall, as 𝑃𝑒,𝑖 increases, the operating current density increases (as shown by
the black markers on the diode curve) and redox shuttle oxidation at the valence band is

                                                           Page 86 of 127

more favored (as shown by the filled pink marker). In both cases, H2 oxidation is occurring
at a rate limited by mass transfer (~77 A m-2).

therefore the Fe(II), permeability increases due to increase in the limiting currents for the
redox shuttle oxidation at the valence band. A permeability ratio of 2 for Fe(II)/Fe(III) in
the coating leads to the largest STH efficiencies due to the increased driving force for
redox shuttle oxidation. However, the effect of this permeability ratio on STH efficiencies
is small because the kinetics of the redox shuttle reactions already promote asymmetry
and selectivity.
The Michigan Team predicted the effects of coating parameters, including thickness,
permeability, and partition coefficient of the redox species, and the boundary layer
thickness on the STH efficiency for an optically thick light absorber to better understand
the effects of mass transport in the coatings and in the bulk solution on the overall
performance of a Z-Scheme reactor (Figure 76). To isolate these effects, other inputs
including electrocatalyst surface coverage, optical properties, and reaction kinetics were
all fixed. The surface coverage of the electrocatalyst was assumed to be 100%, i.e. the
entire surface of the semiconductor, including any cocatalysts, and entirely encapsulated
by the selective coating. The light absorber was assumed to be optically thick, with a band
gap of 1.55 eV, and an optical absorptance of 0.99 for all above bandgap photons. The
kinetic parameters of the redox shuttle (RS, here taken to be Fe(III)/Fe(II)) were assumed
to be j0,RS = 10-7 A cm-2, 𝛼𝑎,𝑅𝑆 = 0.9, 𝛼𝑐,𝑅𝑆 = 0.1, such that the oxidation reaction is more
favorable than the reduction reaction. The kinetic parameters of the HER were extracted
from state-of-the-art Pt performance (j0,H2 = 1 mA cm-2, 𝛼𝑎,𝐻2 = 𝛼𝑐,𝐻2 = 1.28). Finally, the
concentrations of the species in solution were assumed to be a concentration of 5 mM for

                                               Page 88 of 127

the redox shuttle species, an overall pH of 1 and the
dissolved H2 concentration at its solubility limit, 0.78 mM,
corresponding to 1 atm of gaseous H2 in the headspace.
The limiting current densities for all species (Equations
23-25) depend on boundary layer thickness, 𝛿, coating
thickness, 𝑡coat , and species permeabilities (𝑃𝑒,𝑖 ). The
comparison of the anodic limiting current densities
(𝑗lim,RS,a and 𝑗lim,H2,a ) with respect to the short-circuit
current density (𝑗sc ) of the diode dictates in which region
the slab operates: the light-limited region, the kinetics-
limited region, and the mass-transport limited region.

mass-transport-limited behaviors, which are the
