reactions to drive the HER and Fe(II) oxidation, the same optically-thick light absorber will
yield 9.5%, which increases to larger values for smaller boundary layer thicknesses. To
model a conservative case, we assumed that all redox species (Fe(II), Fe(III), H+, H2)
have the same permeability, and therefore the same mass-transfer resistance for a fixed
coating thickness. However, the solubility parameter for these species, which dictates the
ratio of species concentration in the coating to the bulk electrolyte, are still different as
these depend on the effective diffusion coefficient (here assumed to be four orders of
magnitude smaller than the reported bulk diffusion coefficient for each species) as well
as the assumed permeability. As the permeability of the species increases, the maximum
STH efficiency reached for each 𝑃𝑒,𝑖 value increases drastically, going from 0.0052% for
𝑃𝑒,𝑖 = 10-14 m2 s-1 to 4.3% for 𝑃𝑒,𝑖 = 10-10 m2 s-1, reaching our desired range of STH

83a shows results for 𝑡coat = 10-3 nm, where the selectivity towards the desired redox
shuttle oxidation reaction is low (Srxn,VB = 22%) but a positive STH efficiency (1.3%) is
maintained by having a large operating current density of ~10 mA cm-2. As the coating
thickness increases to 𝑡coat = 0.5 nm and 𝑡coat = 10 nm (Figure 83b,c), the total operating
current density 𝑗sc decreases, whereas, the selectivity Srxn,VB increases. Therefore, an
optimum in the STH efficiency of 1.6% is reached for 𝑡coat = 0.5 nm before decreasing to
1.2% for a 10 nm thick coating. Overall, the presence of a coating improves the
performance as compared to the baseline case when no coating is present when 𝑡coat <
1 nm and 𝑃𝑒,𝑖 > 3 x 10-13 m2 s-1, for a boundary layer thickness of 10 μm. STH efficiencies
can be further improved for larger coating thicknesses by varying the relative permeability
of the redox shuttle species to skew toward larger limiting current densities for the desired
reactions. Furthermore, these benefits are intensified when more realistic and symmetric
kinetics, i.e. j0,RS = 0.1 mA cm-2, 𝛼a,RS = 𝛼c,RS = 0.5, were considered for the redox shuttle.
In this case, having the coating dramatically improves the STH efficiency, going from -1%
with no coating, i.e. net H2 consumption, to 0.68% when a coating is present (not shown
on the figure).

Overall, to optimize for the STH efficiency of a particle suspension reactor with selective
coatings, parametric explorations of the interdependencies between the optical, transport,
and kinetic parameters need to be conducted in greater detail.
The effects of independently varying inputs, such as coating parameters including coating
thickness (𝑡coat ) and species permeabilities (𝑃𝑒,𝑖 ), and reactor parameters like the
boundary layer thickness (𝛿), on the STH efficiency were explored above for an optically
thick light-absorber slab. These inputs are important to calculate the limiting current
density 𝑗lim,i,a/c (Equation 26) and the ratio of the anodic limiting current densities (𝑗lim,RS,a
and 𝑗lim,H2,a ) with respect to the short-circuit current density of the photodiode (𝑗sc ) dictates
in which region the slab operates: the light-limited region, the kinetics-limited region, or
the mass-transfer-limited region. To further explore the importance of the limiting current
density ratios on predicted STH efficiency, limiting current densities were parametrized
using the relations listed in Table 7. The 𝑗sc was indirectly varied by changing the number
of light-absorber slabs (indicative of the photocatalyst concentration in the suspension)
from 1 to 100 for a fixed ensemble absorptance of
0.99; as the number of slabs increases, the average Table 7. Ratios defining the limiting
𝑗sc per slab decreases. current densities.

Subtask 4.2. Reactor-Scale Model. The Michigan Team purchased and calibrated a
thermal imaging camera to map the spatiotemporal temperature evolution in a particle
suspension reactor under illumination. They also obtained the reactor prototype that was
developed by the Irvine Team through previous DOE–EERE funding, to be used for
further experimental testing by the Michigan Team.
Experimental measurements of temperature distributions in reactor prototypes.
Temperature profile measurements were conducted using a lab-scale prototype reactor
constructed with an aluminum base for easy conductive heating and polycarbonate walls
for thermal insulation and visualization purposes; two K-type thermocouples were
instrumented at the bottom wall and at the top of the water layer (Figure 86a). For four

surface temperatures, it is clear that solar illumination can induce elevated temperatures
that are favorable for convection (Figure 89b). In contrast to the relatively constant ΔT
observed with the hotplate tests, the temperatures appear to diverge with time, reaching
a steady-state difference of approximately 2 °C. In upcoming tests.
For these experiments, the solar simulator contains a xenon lamp, with a 2” x 2” working
area, and was calibrated with an NLR-traceable silicon reference cell. This was done to
ensure that the irradiance at a working distance of 12” from the output lens was
consistently within 2% of 1 Sun irradiance, i.e. 1 kW/m2. The spectral power output of the
solar simulator is contained almost entirely within the 300 – 1100 nm range of the
spectrum. A filter (AM1.5G) was installed in the beam path to reduce errant peaks from
the xenon lamp and shape the spectrum to more closely match the AM1.5G solar
spectrum. It should be noted that nearly 20% of the energy in the actual AM1.5G solar
spectrum lies above 1100 nm.
For the first set of illumination tests, a 10 cm height was used, and holes were drilled
along one side of the polycarbonate prototype. Eight K-type thermocouples were installed
in the holes such that the probes are roughly in the center of the tube at a designated
height. The holes represent specific distances from
the bottom surface: 0, 0.5, 1, 2, 3, 5, 7, and 9 cm.
Experiments were performed by placing the
prototypes in the center of the solar simulator beam
path such that the bottom surface of the prototype
was ~12” from the output lens of the solar simulator
(Figure 90a). At this distance, the irradiance is
1 ± 0.02 Suns (without water in the beam path). For
one test, all walls of the prototype were covered with
0.25” rubber foam insulation (Figure 90b). This test
was contrasted to a similar test with no insulation Figure 90. (a) Solar simulator

were used to validate

governing equations for this Figure 91. Temperature ranges for two experimental tests,
computational model were reported one with insulation (red) and one without insulation (yellow),
above. Modifications were made to and results from simulations (blue) for which the heat-transfer

with an incident solar flux that
matched the data for the solar simulator. The insulated bottom surface was modeled to
be an acrylic block that was 2.5 cm thick, and with a specific heat capacity of 1,470 J kg-
1
  K-1 and a thermal conductivity of 0.18 W m-1 K-1. The side walls of the polycarbonate
prototype were modeled as insulated thin surfaces. The only free parameter that remains
in the model is the convective heat-transfer coefficient, h, to be modeled at the top surface
of the reactor, which was varied over a range of values (5 – 30 W m-2 K-1). We find that
an h value equal to 30 W m-2 K-1 yielded the best match to the experimentally measured
data (blue and red shaded areas in Figure 91). This value of h is larger than what would
be expected for natural convective heat losses (h ~ 10 W m-2 K-1) due to cooler room air
surrounding a hot top surface of liquid water, which indicates that surface evaporation in
an open top reactor also contributes to heat losses at the top surface. The presence of
evaporative heat losses at the exposed water|air interface enhances temperature
gradients to induce buoyant mixing within the volume of liquid water inside the prototype.
Irradiance was quantified using a Newport 919P-003-10 thermopile measured at a height
corresponding to the top of the reactor prototype. The irradiance was controlled by
modifying the solar simulator aperture until the desired reading was observed on the
thermopile. Based on prior measurements, we expect that the irradiance varies by ~7.5
mW/cm2 across the 10 cm height of the reactor prototype. The following irradiance values
were used for testing: 0.3, 0.5, and 1 Sun (i.e. 1 kW/m2). These values were chosen to
represent a best case (1 Sun), an average value over the course of a day (0.3 Suns), and
an intermediate value (0.5 Suns). Experiments were performed with the top surface of the
reactor exposed to the ambient atmosphere, allowing for evaporation of water and
resulting in more rapid rates of convection.
For each irradiance, temperatures at the bottom surface and within the fluid were
recorded and plotted as a range over a 3-hour time period (Figure 92). The difference in
average temperature by 3 hours was found to be proportional to the incident irradiance:
∆𝑇 ≈ 10 °C/Sun. As expected, the largest temperatures were observed under 1 Sun

                                           Page 97 of 127

illumination, with a range of ~31 °C to ~33 °C after 3
hours. In each case, the highest temperature was
measured at the bottom surface. For 0.3 and 0.5
Suns excitation, very little difference in temperature
was measured among the different points. These
data suggest little convective species and thermal
mixing under illumination conditions expected on
average over the course of a day; however, even a
small amount of mixing can significantly impact
distributions of redox shuttle species.

consisting of a thin, visibly transparent film (DuraSeal

water vapor from the top surface. Over a three-hour Figure 92. Measured temperature

Consequently, the flow behavior were observed to be the same. Condensation formed on

Simulations of light absorption and natural convection. The Michigan Team developed a
two-dimensional, quasi-steady-state model for one reactor compartment to assess the
effects of light absorption and natural convection. A thin black absorber was modeled at
the bottom of the reaction compartment in the absence of photocatalytic particles. The
schematic in Figure 97 shows the two limiting cases that were modeled for the heat-
transfer boundary conditions. A
“worst-case” situation for natural
convection was modeled by
assuming that 90% of the visible
portion of the incident solar spectrum
causes heating of the water layer
from the top. The remaining 10% of
the energy in the visible portion of the
incident sunlight is assumed to be
used for H2 production. The “best-
case” situation was modeled by
assuming that 90% of the visible
portion of the incident solar spectrum Figure 97. Schematic of the worst-case (left) and best-case
 (right) scenarios, modeled to probe the influence of natural
was directly absorbed at the bottom convection on mass transfer.
of the reactor by the black absorber.
In both these cases, incident solar fluxes for the visible (44% of incident spectrum) and
infrared portion (52%) was computed by considering 8 hours illumination. Water was
modeled as a volumetric absorber of the infrared portion of the incident solar flux with an
exponential decay following the Beer–Bouger–Lambert law for this volumetric heat
absorption. An inward species flux of the redox shuttle corresponding to 10% STH
efficiency was applied at the top of the reaction compartment; all the other walls were
modeled as no-flux boundaries for species transport.
Figure 98 shows a comparison of the velocity, temperature, and concentration profiles of
a 1 cm × 1 cm reactor design for the two cases modeled. In the velocity field, the velocity
magnitude for the best-case is about three times larger than that in the worst-case. With
predominant heating from the top, as expected, favorable temperature gradients were not
obtained. Whereas, if most of the incident visible light is absorbed at the bottom, a
favorable temperature gradient of ~1 K/cm was obtained with the bottom surface being
relatively hotter than the top surface. Both these cases resulted in species mixing within
the reaction compartment and natural convection enhanced mass transfer. This result
indicates that for a small reactor size, even without a favorable temperature gradient,

Figure 100. Natural-convection-related parameters for the worst-case and best-case scenarios, and with

because the species concentration profiles are no longer influenced by the changes in
temperature distributions. For the worst-case scenario, although no favorable
temperature difference is predicted for reactor widths lesser than 4 cm, it becomes more
favorable for larger widths of the reactor due to increased temperature differences
between the walls of the reactor and the bottom surface.
For a fixed width of 1 cm, the effect of height of the reactor was also analyzed. With
increasing height of the reactor, the temperature difference turns from being favorable for
natural convection to being unfavorable. This is because a larger portion of the infrared
spectrum is absorbed volumetrically by the fluid (water) as the height increases.
Therefore, there is lesser infrared heat that is absorbed by the black absorber at the
bottom. For both the best-case and the worst-case scenarios, Pe increases linearly and
C* remains constant for heights larger than 2 cm because there is no natural-convection-
induced species mixing beyond this point. Key takeaways from these results are that the
height of the reactor along the path of light absorption is crucial to optimize in order to
maximize the effects of natural convection in this system. Contrary to the expectation that
an increase in system dimensions augments driving forces for natural convection,
because water is an excellent infrared absorber, shallower surfaces could result in
improved “passive mixing” due to natural convection currents.
To summarize, the Michigan Team developed a comprehensive two-dimensional quasi-
steady model of a single reactor compartment to implement coupled mass, momentum,
species, and energy conservation equations. Infrared absorption by water is modeled
assuming Beer–Bouger–Lambert attenuation. Even though photocatalytic particles are
not explicitly modeled, we take into account a constant, volumetric heat-generation term

                                               Page 103 of 127

in the visible spectral region at 400 – 700 nm by assuming a 10% STH efficiency; we
assume that H2 is produced by the photocatalysts only in the visible spectrum of incident
sunlight. Wall boundary conditions are modeled for flow on all four boundary surfaces.
For the energy equation, the bottom of the black absorber is treated as an insulated
surface, whereas the other 3 boundaries were modeled assuming convective heat losses
with a heat-transfer coefficient of h = 12 W m-2 K-1. For the species transport equation,
species flux corresponding to 10% STH efficiency was modeled at the top surface,
whereas zero species flux was imposed on all other surfaces; a volumetric sink was
effectively modeled by constraining the average species concentration to be a fixed value.
Figure 101 shows the two-dimensional spatial
profiles for the temperature distributions and the
concentration for the 1 cm x 1 cm reactor with
wall boundary conditions. A favorable
temperature gradient for natural convection of
0.25 K cm-1 was obtained. Species mixing
improves dramatically with the assistance of
natural convection as compared to the case that
only facilitates diffusive transport.

compared to only diffusion. Figure 102 shows the effect of changing the reactor aspect
ratio, which is defined as the ratio of the reactor width to its height (W/H) on the thermal
and species mixing metrics. Data points colored differently are indicative of the
differences in the fixed and variable parameters in the model. For the data points in blue,
the aspect ratios are >1 and the reactor height is fixed at 1 cm while the reactor width is
varied; for the data points in orange, the aspect ratios are ≤1 and the rector width is fixed
at 1 cm while the reactor height is varied.
Increasing the reactor width, and thus increasing the aspect ratio, results in larger values
for ∆𝑇 and larger average fluid velocities due to convection, although species mixing
largely remains the same yet is consistently enhanced as compared to only having
diffusion-driven species mixing. When natural convection is present, the average
concentration differences between the top and the bottom surfaces only increase slightly

                                       Page 104 of 127

(a) (b)

Figure 102. (a) Temperature difference between the bottom and the top reactor surfaces, ∆𝑇 and (b) non-

for an aspect ratio of >3, because of the more concentrated dead zones, where no mixing
occurs, close to the corners as the reactor width increases.
When the reactor height increases, and thus the aspect ratio decreases, ∆𝑇 becomes
increasingly more unfavorable, i.e. trending downward toward 0, which leads to an
increase in ∆𝐶𝑐onvection . This is because a larger portion of the incident infrared light is
being absorbed volumetrically by the water as the height increases, leaving less light to
be absorbed by the black absorber at the bottom surface. However, ∆𝐶𝑑𝑖𝑓𝑓𝑢𝑠𝑖𝑜𝑛 , which is
the concentration difference between the top and bottom surfaces when diffusion is
considered to be the only mode of species transport, increases more significantly than
∆𝐶𝑐onvection , leading to a decrease in 𝐶 ∗ as the aspect ratio decreases from a value of 1.
Key takeaways from these results are that the aspect ratio is a crucial non-dimensional
parameter to optimize the effects of natural-convection-induced species mixing in this
system, with changes in height most significantly impacting species mixing. Shallow
reaction compartment heights are predicted to result in more enhanced mixing due to
natural convective currents because water is an excellent infrared absorber and strongly
attenuates infrared light from reaching the bottom surface when the height increases.
These results also indicate that with a favorable temperature gradient, species mixing in
the presence of convection always outperforms the scenario of species mixing by
diffusion only.
Simulations to determine allowable concentrations of redox mediator pairs. To track the
effects of convective mixing, we developed a comprehensive two-dimensional transient
model of a two-compartment reactor with coupled mass, momentum, species, and energy
conservation equations. The modeling domain, governing transport equations, initial
conditions, and boundary conditions are shown in Figure 103. The top compartment
(1 cm x 1 cm) models Fe(III) reduction to Fe(II), while the bottom compartment (1 cm x
1 cm) models Fe(II) oxidation to Fe(III), each at a fixed and a specified STH efficiency
value and assuming a day-averaged incident irradiance of 0.33 Suns. For example, at a
1% STH efficiency, the day-averaged volumetric rates of Fe(III) and Fe(II) redox are each
2.78 x 10-6 M/s. Accompanying O2 evolution, H2 evolution, and gas species transport are
not included in the model to keep it tractable and focused on probing effects of convective

                                             Page 105 of 127

  Figure 103. Two dimensional modeling domain comprising of two vertically stacked reaction
  compartments with redox shuttle production and consumption modeled based on fixed STH efficiency
  values. Governing equations, boundary conditions, and initial conditions for mass, momentum,
  species, and energy are presented.
mixing on redox shuttle species transport. A porous membrane is modeled to be
positioned between the two compartments in order to attenuate gas crossover, and a
perfectly black absorber is modeled to be positioned at the bottom of the reactor to serve
as an ideal heat source from absorption of as much transmitted light as possible. Unified
governing transport equations are modeled in the pure fluid and in the porous medium by
introducing the appropriate closure relations that are porosity dependent. For example,
the momentum conservation equation will reduce to the Navier–Stokes equation in the
pure fluid phase when 𝜖𝑝 = 1; and likewise for the other conserved quantities of mass,
energy, and species concentrations.

In order to analyze the effects of convective mixing, we refined our comprehensive two-
dimensional transient model of the two-compartment reactor to implement coupled mass,
momentum, species and energy conservation equations. All conservation laws (mass,
momentum, energy, and species transport) are fully satisfied. Figure 105 shows the
transient evolution of Fe(III) concentrations in a two-compartment reactor (each
compartment is 1 cm x 1 cm) with an initial concentration of 300 mM and an STH
efficiency of 1%. In this figure we show the average concentration of Fe(III) in the top and
the bottom compartments (solid red and green lines, respectively). Also shown are the
overall average redox shuttle concentration for Fe(III) in both the reaction compartments
(cyan solid line), and the minimum redox shuttle concentration (blue solid line). The initial
Fe(III) concentration and total iron species concentration are always equal to 300 mM,
which is a good check to ensure that mass conservation is being fully satisfied. It is
because we apply the same volumetric reaction rate for the redox shuttle consumption
and production in the two compartments.

transmits visible light (Figure 106). With only

initial redox shuttle concentration is ~1 M,
which decreases to >0.2 M upon addition of

Subtask 4.3. Comprehensive Integrated Model. To mimic the light absorption behavior
in a particle suspension reactor, the equivalent circuit model developed by the Michigan
Team was modified. Instead of having all the incoming sunlight absorbed by one optically
thick, planar slab of light absorber, the incoming sunlight was assumed to be absorbed
equally by N absorbers. As a starting point, each absorber was assumed to behave in the
same manner, and incident light intensity was split evenly among these N absorbers
(physically equivalent to perfect scattering of light in the suspension but with optically thick
particles). The short-circuit current density and the reverse saturation current density is
inversely proportional to the number of absorbers. The net STH efficiency was computed
by evaluating the total H2 produced by all N absorbers.

                                         Page 108 of 127

Simulations to determine optimal reactor design and operating conditions. Figure 107a
shows that there is an optimum number of light absorbers to maximize the STH efficiency.
As the number of light absorbers increases, STH efficiency increases up to a point and
then decreases. The initial increase is because short-circuit current densities become
more comparable with mass-transfer-limited current densities for redox shuttle reactions.
When the number of light absorbers are further increased, beyond the optimal value, the
STH efficiency is limited by the electrochemical potential of electrons and holes available
to drive the desired reactions. As j0,H2 decreases, the optimal point shifts to a larger
number of light absorbers. A maximum STH efficiency of 7.93% is predicted for an
exchange current density of 10-4 A m-2 and 500 light absorbers. This value is two orders-
of-magnitude larger than the predicted value for only one light absorber. Figure 107b
shows the predicted H2 selectivity can vary between 5% to 95% depending upon the
number of light absorbers modeled, even with the same kinetic and transport parameters
for the coating. This effect is because of the dependence of driving forces for the reactions
based on the operating current–potential for the diode, which changes with the number

 Figure 107. (a) STH efficiency and (b) HER selectivity as a function of the number of absorbers,

of absorbers modeled. Larger H2 selectivity does not translate to large STH efficiencies,
because H2 oxidation at the valence band impacts STH efficiency.
To further examine the trends shown in Figure 107b, a load-line analysis was performed
considering 500 light absorbers for the two lower values of the H2 exchange current
density (Figure 108). When the exchange current density decreases by one order-of-
magnitude, the H2 current density at the conduction band (filled green marker) decreases
in magnitude, causing a decrease in H2 selectivity seen on Figure 107b. Similarly, the H2
current density at the valence band (open green marker) also decreases in magnitude,
leading to an increase in the STH efficiency for j0,H2 = 10-4 A m-2 as compared to j0,H2 =
10-3 A m-2. Overall, the operating point of the diode shifts toward the condition of
maximum power output, resulting in an increase in the STH efficiency.

Figure 109 shows the calculated STH efficiency as a function of N for a range of j0,H2
values and for the properties corresponding to the thinnest coating (2 nm, 120 min (Table
11)). The H2 reaction was modeled with symmetric charge-transfer coefficients (⍺c,H2 =
⍺a,H2 = 0.5). The optimal STH efficiency depends both on the number of absorbers and
the j0,H2 value modeled. The inset in Figure 109 tabulates the operating current density,
the current densities at the conduction band and valence band for both reactions, and the
operating potential of the device for the maximum STH efficiency (15.6%) obtained with
N = 25 absorbers and j0,H2 = 100 A m-2. At this operating point, selectivity toward the
desired reactions at the valence band and the conduction band are 74.2% and 73.1%,
respectively. At the valence band, the RS and the H2 reactions each occur at mass-
transfer-limited current densities.

to limiting current density for H2 oxidation, jlim,RS,a / jlim,H2,a.

/ jlim,H2,a < 1 results in negative efficiencies, such that more H2 is predicted to be oxidized
than evolved. There is also a distinct difference in the number of absorbers required for
the maximum STH, which depends on the coating used. For the two thinner coatings
considered (2 nm, 120 min and 5 nm, 30 min), the optimal number of absorbers is small
(<27) and this value decreases with a decrease in j0,H2. For these coatings, maximum
STH efficiency occurs when in the presence of a smaller number of absorbers because
the RS limiting current densities are large and therefore, operating near the maximum
power point of the photodiode is possible even at small N. However, for the thickest
coating considered (10 nm, 120 min), the
optimal number of absorbers required is five
times larger (>133) because of the smaller
RS limiting current densities and the lower
exchange current density for the RS (j0,RS for
10 nm and 2 nm coatings are 0.68 and 4.51
A m-2, respectively). Overall, these results

Modeling multiple (N) light absorbers mimics the presence of an ensemble of light
absorbers and is representative of a particle suspension reactor. The equivalent circuit
model developed thus far considered the incoming sunlight to be split equally among all
N absorbers, which meant that each light absorber operated at the same current density
and operating potential. However, it is more realistic to model a height dependence to the
incident light intensity along a series of N light absorbers and therefore, we incorporated
this effect by including a Beer–Bouger–Lambert law absorption profile for the light
intensity (Figure 111). The short-circuit current density, 𝑗sc,𝑖 , and radiative-recombination
current density, 𝑗rr,𝑖 , of the 𝑖 th absorber were calculated as follows.
                 𝜆      𝐺0,AM1.5 (𝜆)
 𝑗sc,𝑖 = 𝑞𝑒 ∫0 bg ℎ𝑐 (exp(−𝛼𝜆 𝑥𝑖 ) − exp(−𝛼𝜆 𝑥𝑖+1 )) 𝑑𝜆
 𝜆 (27)

 𝜆 (28)
 𝑗rr,𝑖 = 𝑞𝑒 ∫0 bg 𝜙̂bb (𝜆) (exp(−𝛼𝜆 𝑥𝑖 ) − exp(−𝛼𝜆 𝑥𝑖+1 )) 𝑑𝜆
where 𝛼𝜆 is the absorption coefficient (in m-1) of the light absorber, 𝐺0,AM1.5 is the incident
AM1.5G solar irradiance spectrum, 𝜙̂bb is the blackbody flux spectrum, and 𝑥𝑖 and 𝑥𝑖+1
are x-coordinates of the 𝑖 th light absorber along the hypothetical optical path. Because
both 𝑗sc,i and 𝑗rr,i are scaled by the non-dimensional absorptance of every light absorber,
the open-circuit potential is the same for each light absorber, irrespective of the value of
N, which is an approximation. Moreover, the overall absorptance of the N-absorber
ensemble was fixed at 90%, irrespective of the value of N. At the valence band and
conduction band both the desired and competing reactions were modeled to occur in
parallel, and the effect of the selective SiOx coatings on mass transfer and reaction
kinetics were determined by the properties reported in Table 11. We also assumed that
light absorbers behave independently from one another and that species transport is rapid
enough that bulk species concentration is maintained everywhere.
STH efficiencies as a function of N and H2 exchange current density are shown in Figure
112a. There is an optimum point with respect to the number of absorbers considered
because for small values of N (<10 for an H2 exchange current density of >1 A m-2) the
operating current densities are limited to the mass-transfer-limited redox shuttle oxidation
currents due to the presence of the selective coatings. However, for larger values of N,
the individual light absorbers are more likely to be limited by the extent of light absorption
and therefore increasingly favor undesired H2 oxidation at the valence band, which is
thermodynamically and kinetically more facile than redox shuttle oxidation and therefore
operates at mass-transfer-limited current densities. This trend is represented in Figure
112b, where negative STH efficiencies are obtained for light absorbers located deeper
along the optical path. This is because of the increasing driving force for the H2 oxidation
reactions at the valence band. The optimal STH efficiency decreases five-fold when
accounting for an exponential attenuation of the incident light as compared to when light
is evenly split among all absorbers, as assumed to mimic a perfectly scattering medium
(Table 13), but increases by up to a factor of 8 at optimal N compared to the N = 1 case.
Table 13 also reveals that among the different coating parameters modeled, the coatings
that yield the largest STH efficiency for the single-absorber case also results in the largest
STH efficiency for the N-absorber model because optimal values are dictated by mass-
transfer-limited current densities for redox shuttle oxidation.

an ensemble of optically thinner slabs (N > 1), the total absorptance by all N slabs, 𝐴total ,
is fixed to a constant value (Equation 29).

material is used. The short-circuit current density, jsc,i, and radiative recombination current
density, j0,rr,i, for each slab are computed by applying the Beer–Bouger–Lambert law, such
that each value decreases exponentially for slabs deeper in the reactor (Equations 31
and 32). This physically indicates bulk-limited recombination, which is consistent with
data obtained from measurements of single Rh-doped SrTiO3 particles by the Talin SNL
EMN Node.

In the present calculations we assumed a bandgap of 1.55 eV and fixed the total
absorptance, 𝐴total = 0.9, i.e. 90% of the incident light is absorbed by the semiconductor
material, irrespective of the value of N. We adopted all other prior formalisms developed
for implementation of competing reactions at the valence and the conduction bands, while
accounting for selective coatings and their effects on mass transfer and reaction kinetics;
all transport and kinetic properties for these coatings were deduced from prior
experimental measurements. We assume that the species are well mixed and that bulk
concentrations are maintained at the surface of all
slabs modeled; however, species concentration
effects on light absorption are not accounted for,
and therefore, physically the slabs are assumed to
be positioned in very close proximity to each other.

less than its optimal value, individual slabs are
limited by mass transfer for desired oxidation of the
redox shuttle; when N is greater than its optimal

at the conduction band on this coating is being consumed by the highly competitive H2
oxidation at the valence band.
Figure 115 shows the effect of varying 𝑗lim,RS,a and 𝑗lim,H2,a with respect to the number of
slabs, for 𝑗lim,RS,c = 10-3 A m-2. When 𝑗lim,RS,a = (𝑗sc /1000), the STH efficiency increases
linearly with the number of slabs, but is overall mass-transfer limited by the small anodic
current densities that can be sustained at the valence band, reaching a maximum of 3.1%
at Nslab = 100. When 𝑗lim,RS,a = (𝑗sc /10), an optimum point in the STH efficiency arises from
both Nslab and 𝑗lim,H2,a . For the smaller 𝑗lim,H2,a values, increasing the number of slabs
results in an increase in the total STH efficiency due to the slabs operating under mass-
transfer-limited conditions. As the number of slabs continues to increase, slabs further
down in the reactor become light limited, resulting in negative STH efficiencies due to the
slab operating under net H2 oxidation. This results in optimum conditions that maximize
STH efficiency, with the largest being 32.8% reached by 𝑗lim,H2,a = (𝑗lim,RS,a /1000) for Nslab

= 50. As 𝑗lim,H2,a increases, H2 oxidation at the valence band becomes more competitive

as well, with the competing RS reduction at the conduction band simply decreasing the
maximum predicted STH efficiency.

These results highlight the importance of the limiting current density behavior on the STH
efficiency, which can also be used to deconvolute the interdependence of the coating
properties and back-calculate the relevant parameters of interest that should be pursued
experimentally.
Figure 116 demonstrates the effect of increasing the number of photoabsorber slabs
when considering different rates of competing reactions. When the competing redox
                            |𝑗     |
shuttle reduction is small ( 𝑙,𝑅𝑆,𝑐 ≤ 0.1), increasing the number of slabs results in an
 𝑗𝑙,𝑅𝑆,𝑎
increase in STH efficiency. This is due to the overall behavior being severely mass-
transfer limited, such that adding more photoabsorbers leads to a multiplicative increase
in the total STH efficiency. The optimum point is reached when the number of
photoabsorbers driving the HER no longer compensates the light-limited slabs on the
bottom of the reactor running the undesired back reactions. This is further confirmed by
the optimum point decreasing with an increasingly competitive H2 oxidation reaction. For
 ≥ 1, no multiplicative effect on the number of slabs considered is observed due to
competing reactions dominating ensemble behavior. Between the two asymmetry
                                       |𝑗𝑙,𝑅𝑆,𝑐 |         |𝑗𝑙,𝐻2 ,𝑎 |
parameters in the limiting currents, and , the larger of the two limits the STH
 𝑗𝑙,𝑅𝑆,𝑎 𝑗𝑙,𝑅𝑆,𝑎
efficiency.

                                          Page 118 of 127

Significant Accomplishments and Conclusions: Significant reproducible
accomplishments include a ≥0.1% quantum yield for H2 evolution and >90% stability over
1 day from suspensions of recently discovered Ir-doped SrTiO3 photocatalyst particles
under 750 – 850 nm near-infrared-light illumination, and several ultrathin oxide coatings
based on TiOx or SiOx deposited on thin-film electrodes that each exhibit ≥95% selectivity
for the desired HER and also the desired OER, over undesired redox reactions with state-
of-the-art redox mediators. These demonstrated performance metrics are significant
because they support a pathway toward particle suspensions that absorb a wide range
of the solar spectrum and with high redox selectivity for water splitting reactions that
should enable a particle suspension reactor with a ≥3% STH efficiency. This award
resulted in no major negative outcomes or pivots requiring reduction in scope. One
challenges that was encountered was a delay in obtaining some of the desired
photocatalyst particles from unfunded collaborators, which taught us that additional
mitigation strategies are needed to ensure a robust supply of materials. In addition, our
End of Project Goal was slightly too ambitious. In total, we published 10 peer-reviewed
journal articles based on this project, including one that describes a technoeconomic
analysis and lifecycle assessment of a new reactor design that was patented as part of
this work and includes efforts by the Breunig LBNL EMN Node and an unfunded
collaborator (Mr. Brian James at Strategic Analysis Inc.).

Path Forward: Future research and development plans include secured follow-on
funding through an awarded DOE BES Energy Frontier Research Center (EFRC), called
Ensembles of Photosynthetic Nanoreactors (EPN), which includes financial support for
the PI, 2 co-PIs, and 3 EMN Node Leads to support basic energy sciences research.
Opportunities for technology transfer and commercialization continue to be explored with
industrial stakeholders. R&D is needed to overcome remaining technical risks that include
the discovery and development of photocatalyst particles that absorb significant amounts
of sunlight and exhibit large STH efficiencies for water spitting. Financial support for
reactor scaleup demonstrations is also needed to assess the real-world feasibility of
reactor designs. Until key advances are made to overcome these gaps, immediate
commercialization seems unlikely.

Project Team and Roles
• Prof. Shane Ardo (Associate Professor, Chemistry, Chemical & Biomolecular
  Engineering, Materials Science & Engineering, University of California Irvine) was the
  lead PI who focused on ensemble photocatalyst measurements, as well as time-
  resolved microwave conductivity spectroscopy and photoelectrochemistry
  measurements.
• Prof. Dan Esposito (Associate Professor, Chemical Engineering, Columbia University)
  was a co-PI who focused on ultrathin oxide coatings and transport modeling, as well
  as high-throughput screening of arrays of materials using scanning electrochemical
  microscopy.
• Prof. Rohini Bala Chandran (Assistant Professor, Mechanical Engineering, University
  of Michigan) was a co-PI who focused on continuum-level multiphysics models and
  simulations of single particles and ensembles, as well as benchtop-scale reactor
  prototypes.
• Dr. Tadashi Ogitsu (Staff Scientist, Lawrence Livermore National Laboratory) was an
  EMN Node Lead who focused on molecular dynamics simulations of ultrathin oxide
  coatings.
• Dr. Katherine Hurst (Senior Scientist, National Laboratory of the Rockies) was an EMN
  Node Lead who focused on atomic layer deposition of ultrathin oxide coatings.
• Dr. A. Alec Talin (Senior Scientist, Sandia National Laboratories) was an EMN Node
  Lead who focused on characterization of single-particle electrical transport and
  photoelectrochemical properties, as well as high-throughput screening of arrays of
  materials using photoluminescence spectroscopy.
• Dr. Hanna Breunig (Staff Scientist, Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory) was an
  EMN Node Lead who focused on lifecycle assessment of photocatalyst particle
  suspension reactor systems.
• Dr. Genevieve Saur (Researcher, National Laboratory of the Rockies) was an EMN
  Node Lead who focused on technoeconomic analysis of photocatalyst particle
  suspension reactor systems.
• Dr. John Gregoire (Research Professor, Applied Physics & Materials Science,
  California Institute of Technology) was a contractor who focused on depositing arrays
  of doped SrTiO3 materials.
• Prof. Takashi Hisatomi (Associate Professor, Research Initiative for Supra-Materials,
  Shinshu University, Japan) was a contractor who focused on synthesizing visible-light-
  absorbing photocatalyst particles.
• Prof. Akihiko Kudo (Professor, Applied Chemistry, Tokyo University of Science,
  Japan) was a contractor who focused on synthesizing visible-light-absorbing
  photocatalyst particles.
• Prof. Kazunari Domen (Professor, Research Initiative for Supra-Materials, Shinshu
  University, Japan) was a collaborator who focused on synthesizing visible-light-
  absorbing photocatalyst particles.

                                    Page 125 of 127

• Dr. Joseph Luther (Senior Research Fellow, National Laboratory of the Rockies) was
  a collaborator who focused on synthesizing visible-light-absorbing photocatalyst
  particles.
• Dr. Junko Yano (Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory) was a collaborator who
  focused on beamline X-ray absorption spectroscopy measurements.
• Mr. Brian James (Vice President, Energy Analysis Services, Strategic Analysis Inc.)
  was a collaborator who focused on technoeconomic analysis of photocatalyst particle
  suspension reactor systems.
